24 Influence of Purāṇas on Dharmaśāstra

[[P913]]

SECTION V

CHAPTER XXIV

Influence of Purāṇas on [[Dharmasastra|Dharmaśāstra]]

Literary works and society act and re-act on each other. The state of Indian society [[&|a]] few centuries before and after Christ, riven as it was by the growing strength and onslaught of Buddhism, Jainism and other dissenting sects and disturbed by invasions of the Greeks, [[Sakas,|Śakas,]] Pahlavas, [[Hūpas|Hūṇas]] and other foreign tribes, gave much food for thought to those who were devoted to the Vedic religion and induced them to write works setting forth new ideologies and practices and effecting a [[re statement|re-statement]] of the ancient Vedic and [[Smrti|Smṛti]] religion. When these works attained a position of authority and eminence, the [[endea vour|endeavour]] of the followers of the Veda was to follow them as far as possible and to adapt their practices and religious rites to the requirements of the Purāṇas. We have to see how the Purāṇas [[Bet|set]] about their task of re-orientation. We must note that current Hindu religious practices, judging from the saṅkalpa made at the beginning of every rite, are meant to confer upon the [[per former|performer]] the rewards declared by Śruti (Veda), [[Smrti|Smṛti]] and [[Purāpas|Purāṇas]] ([[Sruti-smrti-purāpokta-phalaprāptyartham|Śruti-smṛti-purāṇokta-phalaprāptyartham]]). The task was [[two fold,|two-fold,]] viz. (1) to undermine the power and prestige of Buddhism and Jainism and the influence of the numerous philosophical1 schools that had sprung up and (2) to wean away large sections of the masses from the attractive features of Buddhism and to convince them that they could secure in the re-orientated Hindu faith the same benefits, social and spiritual, as Buddhism promised and that the religious principles of the followers of Veda coincided with many of the teachings of Buddhism and were borrowed by Buddhism from Vedic practices. Ultimately Buddhism vanished from the land of its origin. The main causes of this disappearance of Buddhism from Bhārata will be stated at the end of this section, but this much may be said here at once that the [[Purāpas|Purāṇas]] played a substantial role in [[bring ing|bringing]] [[P914]] about the decline and disappearance of Buddhism by emphasizing and assimilating some of the principles and doctrines of Buddha2 such as ahiṁsā, by accepting Buddha himself as an avatāra of Viṣṇu, by adopting vegetarianism as a high form of austerity, by making use of monasteries and asceticism as stated in such smṛtis as those of [[Magu|Manu]] and Yāj.

The Purāṇas set about their task by saying that for the proper understanding of the Veda, knowledge of [[Itihāga|Itihāsa]] and Purāṇa was essential. A famous verse says3 ‘one should [[strongthen|strengthen]] the Veda by (the study and application of) Itihāsa and Purāṇa; the Veda is afraid of the person of little learning (with the thought) that he (the man of small learning) may harm it’. Manu states4 that those brāhmaṇas that have learnt according to the rules (of Veda study) the Veda together with the works that strengthen it are to be understood as [[sistas|śiṣṭas]] and are instrumental in making (the meaning of) the Veda clearly perceptible. The [[Vayu|Vāyu]]5 emphasizes in this connection that that brāhmaṇa who knows the four Vedas together with [[(the six )|(the six)]] ancillary lores and the Upaniṣads would not be a wise man if he did not know Purāṇas. The Upaniṣads drop brief hints about the creation of ākāśa from the one brahma (in Tai. Up. [[II, 1),|II. 1),]] of tejas (Chān. Up. VI. 2. 3), of waters (Chān. Up. VI. 2. 4). The Purāṇas explain at great length the creation and dissolution of the elements (in the order reverse of that of creation) [[o g.|e. g.]] Vāyu [[4,|4.]] 17 ff, Brahma 1-3, Agni 17, [[Brahmānda|Brahmāṇḍa]] II. 3 ff, Kūrma I. [[2,4,7,|2, 4, 7,]] 8 &c. The stories of [[Hariscandra|Hariścandra]] and [[P915]] Naciketas that [[ocour|occur]] in the Ait. Br. and the [[Kathopadiṣad|Kaṭhopaniṣad]] are explained at length in Brahmapurāṇa (chap. 104 and 150 about [[Hariscandra),|Hariścandra),]] in Sabhāparva chap. 12 (for [[Hariscandra)|Hariścandra)]] and in Anuśāsana chap. 91 (for Naciketas). The famous dialogue of Yama and [[Yami|Yamī]] ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 10) is expanded in the [[Narasimha purāpa|Narasiṁha-purāṇa]] (chap. 13. 6-36). The Viṣṇupurāṇa6 (IV. 6. 34 ff) sets out the story of Purūravas and Urvaśī, refers to the hymn in the [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 95 for that story and quotes the first verse of the hymn in a somewhat corrupt form.

But the claims of the extant Purāṇas go far beyond the above viz. as strengtheners of the Veda. The Kūrma7 states: ‘(put) on one side all the Purāṇas together with [[Itihasa (Maha bhārata)|Itihāsa (Mahābhārata)]] and on another the highest Veda; it is this ([[Puranas|Purāṇas]]) that surpasses (the other viz. Veda)’. The Mahābhārata also makes a similar claim. The [[Purāpas|Purāṇas]] appear to claim priority (and even [[equality )|equality)]] with the Veda. In note 1349 passages from the Matsya and other Purāṇas have been cited, stating that [[Brahma|Brahmā]] first thought of the [[Purāpas|Purāṇas]] before all other śāstras and then the Vedas sprang forth from his lips. Several Purāṇas [[ara|are]] spoken of as equal to the Veda (Vedasammita) as in8 Vāyu I. 11, 4. 12, Brahma 1. 29, 245. 4 and 21, Viṣṇu I. 1. 13, [[VI. 8.1%,|VI. 8. 12,]] Padma VI. 282. 116. Further, several Purāṇas claim to have been delivered by some God such as [[Brahma|Brahmā]] (Brahmapurāṇa I. 30) or by the Wind-god [[(Vāyu I. 196 )|(Vāyu I. 196)]]9 or by the avatāras of [[Viśṇu|Viṣṇu]] as in the case of Matsya-purāṇa [[(I. 26 )|(I. 26)]] or Varāha [[(2. 1 3).|(2. 1–3).]] The japa of Vedic texts was deemed to destroy all sins as shown in H. of Dh. vol. IV. pp. 45-50. The Purāṇas also say that reading the Purāṇas or listening to a recitation of them [[P916]] would destroy all sins.10 Vide Vāyu 103. 58, Brahma 175. [[89 90,|89–90,]] [[Matsy 1|Matsya]] 290. 20, [[291, 29|291. 29]] and 31, Viṣṇu VI. 8. 3, 12. Some of the Purāṇas indulge in extravagant praise of themselves, e. g. the [[Varāha-purana|Varāha-purāṇa]] [[( 217. 12-13,|(217. 12–13,]] 217. 15-16) states that reading ten [[chaptors|chapters]] of that Purāṇa confers the merit secured by the performance of [[Agnistoma|Agniṣṭoma]] and Atirātra sacrifices. Vide Brahma [[254, 34-35,|254. 34–35,]] Agni 384. 13-30, [[Devi-Bhāgavata|Devī-Bhāgavata]] XII. 13. 11-17 in a similar strain. Moreover, the Purāṇas dwell upon the superior value and [[officacy,|efficacy,]] as compared with the Vedic sacrifices, of some of the institutions on which they lay emphasis, such as pilgrimages,11 vratas, bhakti. The Padma states (I. 38. 2 and 18) that by merely going to Gayā or by taking a bath in the Phalgu river one secures the reward that the performance of Aśvamedha confers. The Skanda [[proclaimg|proclaims]]12 ‘I have no use for sacrificial rites that are declared by the Veda, that have no life in them, that are within the domain of ignorance and that [[ontail|entail]] injury (to animals). If (a sacrifice) is performed with such inanimate things as fuel-sticks, flowers and [[kusa|kuśa]] grass, the result must be similar (inanimate), since the effect is like the cause’. Vide [[Santiparva|Śāntiparva]] 337 for a story on the discussion between sages and gods about offering in sacrifices merely [[pro ducts|products]] of grains or goat-flesh. Offerings of flesh in sacrifices to gods were made in the [[Rgvedic|Ṛgvedic]] period. But even in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] there are a few verses that indicate that offerings of [[gheo|ghee]] and fuel-sticks were declared to be able to win the favour of gods as much as animal sacrifices might do. ‘Whoever13 offers to (or worships) Agni with a fuel-stick or with an oblation of ghee or [[P917]] with (the study of) the Veda or who performs a good sacrifice with prostrations, for him alone run fleet horses and his is most brilliant fame; and no [[ovil|evil]] whether brought about by gods or by men might reach him from any side’. Another mantra14 [[gays|says]] ‘O Agni! we bring to you an offering accompanied by a [[śkmantra|ṛkmantra]] fashioned (or produced) by our heart. May those mantras be oxen, bulls and cows from you’.

The [[Purāpas|Purāṇas]] only pursue an attitude to Veda and sacrifices that is found in some of the Upaniṣads. The [[Mundakopaniṣad|Muṇḍakopaniṣad]]15 says: ‘one should know two vidyās [[(lores ),|(lores),]] parā (the higher) and [[apara|aparā]] (the lower); the latter comprehends the four Vedas and Phonetics, aphorisms about solemn [[saorifices,|sacrifices,]] grammar, Nirukta (derivation of words), metres, astronomy; while the highest lore is that whereby the Imperishable (Reality) is known’. The same Upaniṣad condemns aparā-vidyā when it says [[“These|‘These]] sacrifices are infirm [[(leaky )|(leaky)]] boats constituted by eighteen [[( persons)|(persons)]] depending on which are declared actions that are inferior; those foolish people who welcome these actions as the highest good become subject again to old age and death’. The [[Kathopaniṣad|Kaṭhopaniṣad]]16 states that what are known as [[avidya|avidyā]] and vidyā are far apart from each other, are contradictory and lead to different results. When Nārada approached Sanatkumāra and requested the latter to teach him, the latter said to him ’tell me what you know and then I shall tell you what is beyond that.’ Then Nārada stated that he [[know|knew]] all the four Vedas, [[Itihasa-purāṇa|Itihāsa-purāṇa]] the fifth Veda and several other [[loros,|lores,]] whereupon Sanat-kumāra told him that the four Vedas and the other lores he had learned were merely a name and then he led Nārada gradually to the understanding of the Highest Self. The Bṛ. Up. [[I, 4, 10|I. 4. 10]] condemns him, who worships [[&|a]] deity thinking he is different from the devatā, that he does not know the truth, that he is like [[&|a]] (sacrificial) animal to the gods. Similarly, in [[P918]] several passages of the Upaniṣads austerities, liberality, [[straight forwardness,|straightforwardness,]] [[ahimsā|ahiṁsā]] and truthfulness are put forward as equal to or superior to the actual performance of the ceremonial of sacrifice ([[yajna|yajña]]); vide Chāndogya III. 17. 4, Praśna I. 15, [[Muṇdaka|Muṇḍaka]] I. 2. 11.

Though in a few passages of the Upaniṣads the knowledge of the Highest Self is put higher than the four Vedas, the [[Upa niṣads|Upaniṣads]] generally treat the Vedas as authoritative and quote Vedic verses in support of their statements. For example, the Ait. Up. II. 5 quotes [[Rgveda IV, 27. 1|Ṛgveda IV. 27. 1]] [[(taduktam-sșiṇā-Garbhe : nu &c.),|(taduktam-ṛṣiṇā-Garbhe nu &c.),]] Praśnop. I. 11 quotes [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. [[164, 12|164. 12]] (pañcapādam pitaram), [[Bș.|Bṛ.]] Up. II. 5. 15, 17 and 19 quote respectively [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 116. 12, I. 117. 22, VI. 47. 18 (rūpam rūpam pratirūpo). The Upaniṣads further emphasize17 that brahmavidyā is to be imparted only to those who are śrotriya [[( who|(who]] have studied the Veda), who engage in their duties and who [[bave|have]] properly [[per formed|performed]] Śirovrata. The [[Bp.|Bṛ.]]18 Up. shows that Veda study, sacrifices, charity &c. are preparations for the knowledge of brahma: ‘Brāhmaṇas [[(and others )|(and others)]] desire to know this [[( great|(great]] Self) by study of the Veda, by sacrifices, gifts, tapas, fasting’. These passages make it clear that study of the Veda and [[per formance|performance]] of sacrifices enjoined by it are accepted by the [[Upa niṣads|Upaniṣads]] as preparatory and as cleansing the mind of its lower passions and as making a person worthy of receiving the [[know ledge|knowledge]] of the highest truth about the One Supreme Spirit and that the Upaniṣads do not altogether condemn and give up the [[Vodas|Vedas]] and [[sacrifices,|sacrifices.]]

The [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] adopt, in spite of the claims made by them here and there about their priority to the Vedas, about their own value and efficacy, the same attitude towards the Veda as the Upaniṣads do. They treat the Vedas as authoritative and enjoin [[P919]] the employment of Vedic mantras in many rites. The author contributed a paper to the Dr. Kunhan Raja Presentation volume pp. 5-8 on the ‘Vedic mantras and legends employed in Purāṇas’ citing illustrations from the Brahmapurāṇa. A few illustrations from other Purāṇas may be set out here. The [[Matayapurāṇa|Matsyapurāṇa]] (chap. 93) when describing the procedure of the homa to the nine planets prescribes nine Vedic mantras, five of which are different from those provided by Yāj. (I. 300-301). Vide p. 750 above for a comparative table of the two sets of mantras. In the [[Udvahatattva|Udvāhatattva]]19 Raghunandana remarks that the mantras ‘ā [[krspeda’|kṛṣṇena’]] and others are common to persons following the four Vedas and that Bhavadevabhaṭṭa held the same view. Matsya prescribes that when inauspicious birds (like an owl) or animals cry near a house or enter it, a homa should be performed, or five brāhmaṇas should engage in a japa20 of the hymn beginning with [[‘Duvāḥ|‘Devāḥ]] kapota’ (Ṛg. X. 165. 1-5). In describing the whole procedure of the establishment of images of gods or the liṅga (of Śiva), the Matsya [[(chapter 265 )|(chapter 265)]] prescribes numerous Vedic verses and [[hymna|hymns]] for the different parts of the ceremony. For example, for bringing about the purification of the image four mantras are [[presoribed|prescribed]] viz. Ṛg. [[VII49.|VII. 49.]] 1-3 and X. 9. 1; in providing for the placing of a jar full of water near the head of the bed on which the image is to be placed, two mantras [[‘Āpo devi’|‘Āpo devī’]] (Vāj. S. 12. 35, Tai. S. [[IV. 2.32 )|IV. 2. 32)]] [[andāpo|and ‘āpo]] asmān [[mātarah’|mātaraḥ’]] (Ṛg. X. [[17.10 )|17. 10)]] are to be recited. Matsya [[( 265.24–29)|(265. 24–29)]] prescribes for the japa of several sūktas (hymns) by four door-keepers learned in the Vedas that are to stand in the four main [[direo tions.|directions.]] The Agnipurāṇa (41. 6-8) providing for the laying down of baked brick or stones in building a temple [[proscribes|prescribes]] the [[Tecital|recital]] of many Vedic mantras, viz. [[Kg.|Ṛg.]] X. 9. 1-3, Ṛg. X. 9. 4, Ṛg. IX. 58. 1-4, Pāvamānī verses (either Ṛg. IX. 1. 1-10 or verses from Ṛg. IX), Ṛg. I. 24. 15, Ṛg. IV. 31. 1, Vāj. S. IV. 36 [[(Varupasya),|(Varuṇasya),]] Ṛg. IV. 40. 5 and the [[Srīgākta|Śrīsūkta]] (of 29 [[Verses|verses]] [[P920]] beginning with [[‘Hiraṇyavarṇam harinim’).|‘Hiraṇyavarṇām hariṇīm’).]] The [[Nāradiya purāṇa|Nāradīya-purāṇa]] [[( II.|(II.]] 73. 83-90) contains at end of each of the verses parts of [[vedio|vedic]] prayer, which [[ooour|occur]] in Ṛg. VII. 66. 16, Tai. Ār. IV. 4. 2-5 and [[Vaj.|Vāj.]] S. 36. 24; Bhāgavata I. 2. 21 (bhidyate &c.) is taken from [[Mundaka|Muṇḍaka]] Up. II. 2. 8.

The Purāṇas not only prescribe Vedic mantras for various purposes, but suggest the employment of numerous [[Paurāpika|Paurāṇika]] mantras. It appears that Paurāṇika mantras came to be employed along with Vedic mantras in religious rites even of Brāhmaṇas at the beginning of the Christian era or within [[&|a]] few centuries after Christ. Yāj. [[I, 229|I. 229]] prescribes that the [[Visve Deyas|Viśve Devas]] should be invited to come to a śrāddha with the [[çk,|ṛk,]] ‘O [[Visve-Devas!|Viśve-Devas!]] Come, listen to this my call, sit down on these kuśas’ (Ṛg. II. 41. 13). On this the Mitākṣarā (about 1100 A. D.) remarks that along with the Vedic mantra mentioned by Yāj. [[&|a]] smārta mantra should also be employed viz. the mantra21 [[‘āgacobantu|‘āgacchantu]] mahābhāgā,’ which occurs in Skanda and [[Garuḍa purāṇa.|Garuḍa-purāṇa.]] Vide H. of Dh. vol. IV. p. 440 note 984 for the [[ascrip tion|ascription]] of this verse to various authorities. The [[Vayupurāṇa|Vāyupurāṇa]]22 prescribes that the mantra ‘adoration to the [[devatūs,|devatās,]] to [[pits,|pitṛs,]] to the great Yogins, to [[stadha|svadhā]] and svāhā; they are always present’ should be recited thrice at the beginning and at the end of [[brāddha|śrāddha]] and at the time of offering [[pindus;|piṇḍas;]] the [[pitļa|pitṛs]] [[quiokly|quickly]] come when the mantra is repeated and goblins run away; this mantra protects the [[pitrs|pitṛs]] in all three worlds’. This mantra is styled ‘Saptārcis’ (having seven flames) in Vāyu 74. 20, [[Brah mānda III.|Brahmāṇḍa III.]] 11. 30 and in Viṣṇudharmottara [[I, 140.|I. 140.]] 68 and by Hemādri on śrāddha pp. 1079 and 1208, who notes that it occurs in seven [[Puranas.|Purāṇas.]] In chap. 206 of the Agni in the procedure of offering arghya to the star Agastya (Canopus) [[Rg. I|Ṛg. I.]] 179. 6 has been adopted as verse 13.

[[P921]] Not only are mantras from Vedic [[Samhitay|Saṁhitās]] prescribed by the Purāṇas for certain rites, but Upaniṣad passages also [[some times|sometimes]] with slight variance are bodily transferred into some of the Purāṇas. For example, Kūrma II. 9. 12, 13 and 18 embody Tai. Up. II. 4 [[(yato vāco nivartante ),|(yato vāco nivartante),]] [[Syetaśvataropa. III, 8|Śvetāśvataropa. III. 8]] [[(vedāham-etam puruşam )|(vedāham-etam puruṣam)]] and VI. 11 ’eko devaḥ’. The [[Vigpu purāṇa|Viṣṇupurāṇa]] VI. 5. 65 is ‘dve vidye veditavye iti [[catharyani|cātharvaṇī]] śrutiḥ’ and contains a [[passage:of|passage of]] the [[Mundaka|Muṇḍaka]] Up. [[I.1.4|I. 1. 4]] (vide note 1461 above). The Vāyu 20. 5 [[(prapavo|(praṇavo]] dhanuḥ) and 20. 28 (Ajām-ekām) are respectively [[Mupdakopa. II, 2.4|Muṇḍakopa. II. 2. 4]] and Śvetāśvatara-up. IV. 5. Vāyu 14. 13 (sarvataḥ pāṇipādāntam) is almost the same as [[Svetaśyatara|Śvetāśvatara]] Up. III. 16 and Vāmana [[47, 64-65|47. 64–65]] has the same verse. [[Vamana|Vāmana]] 47. 67 is almost the [[game|same]] as Ṛg. I. 10. 1.

This gives rise to interesting questions. The śūdras had no right to study the Veda. But as a matter of fact the Purāṇas contain as exemplified above a good many Vedic mantras. It is stated in the Bhāgavata23 ‘women, śūdras and brāhmaṇas in name only are beyond the pale of the three Vedas; therefore the sage (Vyāsa) composed through compassion for them the [[Bharata|Bhārata]] tale’. The [[Devibhagavata|Devībhāgavata]] states ‘study of the [[Voda|Veda]] [[P922]] is not accepted for women, [[tūdras|śūdras]] and [[brahmanas|brāhmaṇas]] in name only and [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] are compiled for the purpose of benefitting them’. From this it follows that in the case of [[gūdras|śūdras]] the listening to the [[Bharata|Bhārata]] was deemed to bring about the same results that the Veda does for [[drijas|dvijas]] and that even the śūdra may acquire the knowledge of the Self (and mokṣa) from the Bhārata.

Though the [[brāhmapas|brāhmaṇas]] in the 5th and following centuries A. D. wanted to placate the śūdras who were probably a majority of the whole people and to wean them away from Buddhism, they still kept a distinction between dvijas and śūdras and the only concessions made [[wore|were]] that the śūdras could worship in the same way as dvijas did and that they could have mantras (Paurāṇika) in their rites and ceremonies. For example, in allowing bhasma-snāna [[(smoaring|(smearing]] ashes on the [[body )|body)]] the Padma [[(IV. 110. 236-289)|(IV. 110. 286-289)]] provides Vedic mantras for men of the three varṇas but Paurāṇika mantras only (Padma IV. 110. 290-293) for śūdras. The Padma24 further provides that śūdras could not perform prāṇāyāma or utter the sacred syllable ‘om’ but that they should substitute dhyāna in place of prāṇāyāma and ‘Śiva’ in place of ‘om’.

Gradually in some matters the procedure provided in the [[Purāpas|Purāṇas]] came to supersede the ancient [[Vodic|Vedic]] procedure [[pre scribed|prescribed]] for them. Aparārka states (on p. 14) that in Devapūjā one should follow the procedure prescribed in the [[Narasimha purāṇa|Narasiṁhapurāṇa]]25 and the like and not the procedure of the [[Pasupatas|Pāśupatas]] or [[Pancarātras|Pāñcarātras]] and (on p. 15) he says the same about the [[con secration|consecration]] of temples,26 images and the [[like,|like.]]

The [[Narasimhapurāṇa|Narasiṁhapurāṇa]] (chap. 63. 5-6) says that the mantra [[*om|‘om]] namo Nārāyaṇāya’ enables one to secure all objects and japa of it frees a man from all [[sing|sins]] and leads him to absorption into Viṣṇu.27

[[P923]] The Agnipurāṇa [[(chap, 818) destribes|(chap. 218) describes]] the procedure of the king’s coronation and (chapter 219) sets out the [[Pauranika|Paurāṇika]] mantras (about 70) employed at the coronation. Similarly, the [[Vigṇudharmottara|Viṣṇudharmottara]] (II. 21) describes the procedure of coronation with Vedic mantras and [[(in II. 22 )|(in II. 22)]] with 184 Paurāṇika [[Verses|verses]] invoking various gods, minor deities, sages, rivers [[&o.|&c.]] Medieval digests like the [[Rājanitiprakāśa|Rājanītiprakāśa]] (pp. 49–83), Nītimayūkha (pp. 1-4), Rājadharmakaustubha (pp. 318-363) describe the combined coronation procedure of Vedic and [[Paurāpika|Paurāṇika]] mantras from the [[Visnu-dharmottara|Viṣṇu-dharmottara]] (vide pp. 78-79 of the [[H. of Dh,|H. of Dh.]] vol. III. for details). The [[Rājanitiprakāśa|Rājanītiprakāśa]] (pp. 430-433) prescribes numerous mantras derived from the [[Visaudharmottara|Viṣṇudharmottara]] as prayers and as blessings.

The Padmapurāṇa28 narrates the interesting story of a person called Dhanaśarmā whose father followed only the Vedic path [[(śrauta-raārga),|(śrauta-mārga),]] who did not engage in such Paurāṇika prescriptions as Vaiśākhasnāna and who therefore became a horrible and distressed preta. Some of the verses are very [[inter esting.|interesting.]] ‘I performed in my ignorance only Vedic rites and I never observed [[Vaisakhasnāna|Vaiśākhasnāna]] in honour of God Mādhava [[(Vispu),|(Viṣṇu),]] nor observed a single Vaiśākha Full Moon vrata which is like an axe for cutting the tree of sins that afflicts one like a conflagration fed by [[the- fuel|the fuel]] of sinful deeds &c.; to one who studies many śāstras and several Vedas with their extensive ancillary [[Literature,|literature,]] learning does not come, if he has not studied Purāṇas’. This shows what importance came to be attached to Purāṇas not only for śūdras but even for brāhmaṇas who performed the Vedic rites prescribed for them.

The influence of the [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] went on increasing gradually. At first it was said29 that the dharma understood from the Veda was the highest, while the dharma declared in the Purāṇas [[P924]] and the like was inferior. This gradually changed and dharma was said to be of three kinds, Vaidika, [[Tantrika|Tāntrika]] and [[Mista|Miśra]] and it was said in the Bhāgavata30 and the Padma that one may worship god [[Vispu|Viṣṇu]] in any one of the three ways that one desired. The Padma adds that the Vaidika and Miśraka methods are declared to be proper for [[brāhmanas|brāhmaṇas]] and the like, while the [[Tantrika|Tāntrika]] method of worship is meant for a devotee of Viṣṇu and also for śūdras. The [[Devibhāgavata|Devībhāgavata]] (XI. 1. 21–23) states that [[Sruti|Śruti]] (Veda) and [[Smrti|Smṛti]] are the eyes of dharma and Purāṇa its heart, and that that is dharma which is declared by these three and that dharma [[oan|can]] be found nowhere else than in these, that in [[Purāpas|Purāṇas]] sometimes what is found in Tantras is put forward [[&8|as]] dharma, but one should not accept that.

The Bhaviṣya (Brāhmaparva [[I, 43-47)|I. 43–47)]] in a dialogue between [[Satānika|Śatānīka]] and [[Sumantu)|Sumantu]] first enumerates the 18 dharma-śāstras from Manu to Atri, states that the Vedas, the śāstras of Manu and [[otbers|others]] and the [[angas|aṅgas]] are promulgated for the three [[varpas|varṇas]] and not for the benefit of śūdras, that śūdras appear to be very much helpless; how can they be able to secure the four puruṣārthas? They are devoid of [[agama|āgama]] (traditional [[loro);|lore);]] what traditional lore was declared for them by the wise among the brāhmaṇas for enabling them to secure the three viz. dharma, artha and [[kama?|kāma?]] Sumantu replies:31 listen to the [[Dharma Śāstras|Dharmaśāstras]] that were declared by wise [[mon|men]] for the benefit of all four [[Varpas|Varṇas]] and specially for Śūdras viz. the 18 Purāṇas, the life of Rāma of the Raghu race (i. e. the Rāmāyaṇa) and the Bhārata [[P925]] declared by Parāśara’s son [[(Vyasa);|(Vyāsa);]] the compassionate [[Vyasa|Vyāsa]] composed a śāstra for the benefit of all four [[Varnas|Varṇas]] in which he comprehended the entire meaning of the Veda and the [[Dharmen sāstras;|Dharmaśāstras;]] it is an unparalleled boat for all that are engulfed in saṁsāra. This makes it clear that the Purāṇas, the [[Mahābhā. rata|Mahābhārata]] and the Rāmāyaṇa embody pristine traditions and [[thoughts:|thoughts]] and were deemed to have been composed as the instruments of the education and enlightenment of the common people. As [[&|a]] matter of fact we find that some Purāṇas like Agni, Matsya, Viṣṇudharmottara are [[encyclopaedio|encyclopaedic]] and include treatises on politics and government, on law, medicine, astronomy, [[astro logy,|astrology,]] poetry, music, [[soulpture|sculpture]] &c. They illustrate India’s life and character as a country and exemplify [[Bharata’s|Bhārata’s]] achievements, weaknesses and shortcomings. Two questions that arise are: (1) could the [[Pusāpas|Purāṇas]] including the Vedic mantras quoted therein be read by the śūdras themselves; [[(%)|(2)]] supposing that Vedic mantras could not be read by the śūdras, could they, [[it’|if]] they were able to do so, by themselves read the purāṇas without a [[brāhmana’s|brāhmaṇa’s]] help. All writers of digests and [[commentarios|commentaries]] were agreed that śūdras could not read or listen to the Vedic mantras contained in the Purāṇas (which being meant for the benefit of all [[yarpas|varṇas]] contained Vedic mantras also), but only those that belonged to the three upper classes. Some writers, however, were agreeable to śūdras reciting Paurāṇika mantras in religious rites, relying on a passage of the Padmapurāṇa. But others like [[Kamalakarabhatta,|Kamalākarabhaṭṭa,]] author of the [[Nirnayasindhu|Nirṇayasindhu]] and [[Sūdrakamalakara,|Śūdrakamalākara,]] held relying on verses of the Bhaviṣya that Paurāṇika mantras alone were to be used by a brāhmaṇa [[in a,|in a]] religious rite for a śūdra, that the śūdra was only to listen to the reading of a purāṇa by a [[brahmana|brāhmaṇa]] reader. There was a third view held by [[Sridatta|Śrīdatta]] and others that a śūdra could recite a paurāṇika mantra, but he should not himself read the Purāṇas and should only listen to the reading of Purāṇas by a [[brāhmane|brāhmaṇa.]] In the times of the Dharmasūtras the only mantras employed were mostly Vedic and therefore in the case of śūdras Gautama32 provided that the śūdras were allowed the [[alter native|alternative]] of saying ’namaḥ’ in place of a Vedic mantra. In the centuries preceding Christ the śūdras would naturally have been attracted to Buddha’s teaching as it was addressed to all including śūdras. Comparatively early orthodox writers like [[P926]] Kumārila [[know|knew]] that the śūdras formed the largest number of professed Buddhists when he says33 ’the dicta of [[Sakya|Śākya]] and others were all opposed, except a [[fow|few]] relating to self-restraint, charity and the like, to all the fourteen [[gources|sources]] of learning, were promulgated by Buddha and others that [[bad|had]] strayed from the path of the three Vedas and did acts contrary (to the Veda) and that those dicta were presented by them to those who were deluded, who were outside the pale of the three Vedas, who mostly comprised the 4th varṇa (i. e. śūdra) and those that had lost caste’. Therefore, the learned brāhmaṇas who wanted to wean sections of the masses (including śūdras) away from Buddhist teachings composed [[now|new]] Paurāṇika mantras by the thousand and employed them in all religious rites like śrāddhas, vratas &c. It was, therefore, that earlier nibandha writers like [[Śridatta|Śrīdatta]] were prepared to allow śūdras to recite Paurāṇika34 mantras. But when centuries had elapsed after Buddhism had disappeared from India, orthodox [[brābmana|brāhmaṇa]] writers like [[Kamalakara|Kamalākara]]35 (who wrote his N. S. in 1612 A. D.) showed a stiffer attitude by confining śūdras merely to the listening to Purāṇas read by a brāhmaṇa and by not allowing them even to recite a Paurāṇika mantra. It may be noted that the [[Nara simhapurāṇa|Narasiṁhapurāṇa]] in laying down the duties of śūdras provides that they should listen to the reading of purāṇas by a [[brābmapa|brāhmaṇa]] [[P927]] (reader) and should worship [[Narasimha|Narasiṁha]] (avatāra of [[Viṣṇu),|Viṣṇu).]] The relation of Śruti, [[smrti|Smṛti]] and [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] and their [[spheros|spheres]] of application are summed up in the [[Naradiyapurāṇa|Nāradīyapurāṇa]] [[as follows:|as follows:—]] “The Veda exists in different forms; there is the Veda which has the performance of sacrificial rites (as its sphere); [[smrtis ara|smṛtis are]] the Veda for the householder’s stage; both those are centered in (or supported by) the [[Puranas.|Purāṇas.]] Just as this wonderful world sprang from the ancient Being (God), there is no doubt that all this literature arose from the [[Purāṇag.|Purāṇas.]] I hold that the meaning (or purpose) of the Purāṇas is more extensive (or superior to) than the meaning (or purpose) of the Veda. All the [[Vedai|Vedas]] always rest on the Purāṇas; the Veda is afraid of the man of little learning (with the thought that) that man may harm it. The Veda does not deal with the movements of the [[planeta,|planets,]] nor does it contain correct calculations about the proper [[times ( for|times (for]] religious acts), nor does it deal with the [[tithivrddhi|tithivṛddhi]] or [[tithikaya,|tithikṣaya,]] nor with the determination of the [[partans|parvans]] (amāvāsyā, [[pūrnima|pūrṇimā]] &c.) or eclipses. Determinations about these were formerly made in Itihāsa and Purāṇas. What is not seen in the Vedas is all noticed in the [[Smstis;|Smṛtis;]] what is [[nog geen|not seen]] in both is declared in the [[Puranas.|Purāṇas.]] What is declared by the Vedas and what is declared by the auxiliary lores [[–|—]] it is [[Voda|Veda]] that is declared by [[Smśtis|Smṛtis]] and [[Purāpas.|Purāṇas.]] The person who looks upon [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] in any other light would be born as a lower animal’.36 The [[Nāradiyapurāṇa|Nāradīyapurāṇa]]37 further states ’the merit acquired by those wicked men who speak about Purāṇas as arthavādas [[(more|(mere]] laudatory38 or condemnatory statements) are destroyed and [[P928]] the wicked man who regards as arthavādas the [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] that are the means of uprooting [[( the|(the]] evil effects of) all [[acta,|acts,]] reaches Hell’.

The Purāṇas introduced several striking changes in the religious rites, practices and ideals of the people. The most characteristic thought and the keynote of the Purāṇas [[ia|is]] to declare how great rewards and results could be secured with little effort. The Viṣṇupurāṇa (VI. 2) narrates how sages approached [[Vyāga|Vyāsa]] with the question [[in|‘in]] what age does a little [[ildharma|dharma]] yield very great rewards’? Vyāsa was bathing in the Ganges; he came out, uttered ‘śūdra is good and Kali is good’ and then again plunged into the river; then he again came out and said ‘well done, O [[Śūdra!|śūdra!]] you are blessed’; he again plunged into the river, came out and said ‘women are good and blessed; who is more blessed than they’. When he finished his bath and performed his morning rites, the sages asked him to explain what he meant by calling Kali, śūdras and women good and blessed. He replied: ‘a man secures in a [[sipgle|single]] day and night in Kali age as much reward of [[lapus,|tapas,]] celibacy and japa as is obtained in ten years in [[Kr̥ta|Kṛta]] age, in one year in Tretā and in a month in [[Dyāpara;|Dvāpara;]] therefore, I spoke of Kali as good; in Kali age a man secures merely by the glorification or incessant repetition of the name of Keśava what he would secure by deep meditation in Kṛta, by sacrifices in Tretā, and by worship in Dvāpara; I am pleased with Kali because a man secures great eminence of dharma with a little effort. Persons of the three higher varṇas have to study the Vedas after observing many [[atrict|strict]] rules, then they have to perform sacrifices which require wealth; they incur sin if they do not perform their duties properly; they cannot eat and drink as they please, but are dependent on the observance of many rules as to food &c.; dvijas secure higher worlds after great trouble; the śūdra secures his worlds by serving the [[thres Varnas,|three varṇas,]] he has the right to offer the pākayajñas (without mantras) and therefore he is more blessed than a dvija. He has not to observe strict rules about proper and disallowed food or drink and therefore he [[wag|was]] [[P929]] declared ‘good’ by me. A woman by serving her husband in thought, word, and deed secures with [[legs|less]] trouble the same worlds that her husband secures with great effort and trouble and therefore I said a third time about women that they were blessed. The [[acquistion|acquisition]] of dharma is secured with small trouble in Kali age by men who wash off all their sins by the water in the form of the qualities of their soul; śūdras do the same by being intent on service to dvijas and women also secure the [[Bame|same]] without trouble by service to their husbands. Therefore all these three are regarded by me as most blessed.’39 The [[Brahmapurana|Brahmapurāṇa]] chapter 229 verses 62-80 are identical with [[Viṣṇu purana|Viṣṇu-purāṇa]] VI. 2. 15-30 and 34-36. The [[Viṣṇupurana|Viṣṇupurāṇa]] emphasizes that each one must do one’s duty in the society in which one is born or one’s duty which one has undertaken, that, if a person does this, he reaches the [[game|same]] higher worlds, whether he be a brāhmaṇa or a [[gūdra.|śūdra.]] This doctrine is the same as taught in the [[Bhagavadgita|Bhagavadgītā]]40 18. 45 and 46 ‘a person secures the highest perfection (final emancipation) by being intent on carrying out the duties appropriate to him; man secures perfection (or [[bllag)|bliss)]] by worshipping with the performance of his peculiar duties [[( not|(not]] with flowers and the like or by words) Him from whom all beings proceed and by whom all this (world) is enveloped’. Ancient works like the Vedas, Jaimini’s sūtras on [[Mimānsā|Mīmāṁsā]] and the [[Vedāntasutras|Vedāntasūtras]] did not consider or discuss how women or śūdras were to secure higher spiritual life and final Beatitude. The [[Vedantasutra|Vedāntasūtra]] [[(I|(I.]] 3. 34-38) denies to the śūdra the right to study the Veda and the Upaniṣads. Buddha’s teaching held out the same promise of liberation from suffering [[P930]] to all men irrespective of class or [[oaste|caste]] and was therefore most attractive to śūdras. The [[Bhagavadgitā|Bhagavadgītā]] and the [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] changed the whole outlook of Indian society, high or low, and promised the same higher spiritual life or worlds to all who [[dia|did]] their work under a [[senge|sense]] of social duty, did not hanker after mundane rewards and brought all their actions, in whatever avocation they might be [[ongaged,|engaged,]] as an offering to God. In the [[Padmapurāpa|Padmapurāṇa]]41 Vyāsa is made to say to [[Yudhisthira:|Yudhiṣṭhira:]] “It is not possible to observe in the Kali age the rules of Dharma laid down by Manu and by the Vedas; the one thing which one should do is to observe a fast on Ekādaśī in both fortnights [[(of a month ),|(of a month),]] which is an easy means (that) requires little wealth, that entails little trouble, but yields great rewards, that is the very essence (of the teachings) of all Purāṇas; he should be pure and on [[Dyādaśī|Dvādaśī]] after worshipping Keśava with flowers [[be|he]] should first feed [[brāhmanas|brāhmaṇas]] and then himself take his meal; those who desire to secure heaven should perform this vrata throughout their lives; even persons of evil conduct, the greatest sinners devoid of dharma, do not go to Yama [[( do|(do]] not fall into hell) if they fast on [[Ekādasi."|Ekādaśī.”]] The [[Sūtasarhitā|Sūtasaṁhitā]] states ’effort for acquiring true knowledge (of the Self) is meant for all (for persons even lower than śūdras), that effort made by explaining in a different language (than Sanskrit) and by the lapse of enough time will tend to the good [[( of|(of]] the lowest)’. This clearly shows how the Purāṇas put before all people easy ways whereby they could attain bliss in the [[Hereafter,|Hereafter.]]

The Baud.42 Dh. S., Manu and [[Vasistha|Vasiṣṭha]] emphasize that one should not invite a large company of brāhmaṇas at a śrāddha, because a large company destroys these five [[(advan tages)|(advantages)]] viz. showing proper respect to invitees, propriety of place and time, cleanliness and the securing of worthy brāhmaṇas and [[P931]] it was further provided by the [[Anusagana-parva|Anuśāsana-parva]]43 and others that one should not go deep into examining the learning, family and character of brāhmaṇas in a rite for the gods, but in rites for the [[Manog|Manes]] close examination as to these matters is proper (or justified). The Purāṇas went against both those [[prescrip tions.|prescriptions.]] They are not tired of recommending again and again profuse expense in [[grāddhas|śrāddhas]] and [[condemaning|condemning]] stinginess (lit. roguishness in spending money) in śrāddhas and also in such other matters as the observances of [[Ekādasi.|Ekādaśī.]] For example, the Viṣṇupurāṇa44 quotes nine verses (III. 14. 22-30) as uttered by the pitṛs, two of which may be translated here ‘Would that [[&|a]] wise and blessed person be born in our family who not [[indulg ing|indulging]] in stinginess in spending wealth will offer [[pindas|piṇḍas]] to us and who would donate to brāhmaṇas for our sake jewels, clothes, a large conveyance, wealth and all enjoyments if he has riches’! Padma45 recommends that avoiding [[stinginoss|stinginess]] brings pleasure to the [[pitys.|pitṛs.]] The Matsya (56. 11) prescribes that one should not show stinginess in the [[Kļspāstamivrata.|Kṛṣṇāṣṭamīvrata.]] The Padma says that that bad man who being possessed of wealth celebrates the jāgara on Ekādaśī in a close-fisted way loses his soul. The Brahmapurāṇa46 says in a general way that whoever does a religious act with stinginess is a sinner.

Manu (III. 149) provides that ‘in rites in honour of gods one who knows dharma should not [[oritically|critically]] examine the [[brāhmapas|brāhmaṇas]] to be invited for dinner, but in a rite for ancestors one should carefully investigate [[fitness of)| (the fitness of)]] [[brāhmadas’.|brāhmaṇas’.]] This does not mean that in rites for gods any one may be invited. We have to observe the general rule of Manu [[(II.|(III.]] 128) that donors should give only to a man who has studied the Veda [[P932]] [[dinger|dinner]] in rites for gods or [[pitra.|pitṛs.]] So what Manu III. 149 means is that deep investigation of ancestry [[&o,|&c.]] is not necessary in rites for [[gods,|gods.]]

It is provided by the Vāyu47 that there is to be no [[exami. nation|examination]] of the qualities and character of the brāhmaṇas at Gayā, and the Varāhapurāṇa provides that all [[brāhmanas|brāhmaṇas]] of Mathurā are like gods and that a [[brāhmana|brāhmaṇa]] of Mathurā not knowing even a [[k|ṛk]] verse is superior to a brāhmaṇa of another place that has [[atudied|studied]] the four Vedas. Vide H. of Dh. vol. IV. pp. 579, 670 for details of [[Gaya|Gayā]] and Mathurā brāhmaṇas. The Padmapurāṇa and the Skanda (Kāśīkhaṇḍa 6. 56-57) recommend that at [[tirthas|tīrthas]] [[(Sacred|(sacred]] places) one should not engage in investigation [[the worth)|(of the worth)]] of [[brābmapas,|brāhmaṇas,]] and that Manu declares that brāhmaṇas appearing (at a tīrtha) and desiring food should be fed.48

It is not unlikely that the above passages from the Vāyu, [[Varāba|Varāha]] and Padma are later interpolations. When Buddhism was flourishing large companies of Buddhist monks were [[fod|fed]] by the people. When Buddhism declined and disappeared from India after the 12th or 13th century A. D. people came to believe that there was great merit in feeding poor brāhmaṇas just as in former times people fed monks and the Purāṇas might have only echoed and emphasized the general sentiments of the people. If the people in general had not come to believe in this, the author feels that no amount of interpolations or insistence by the [[so called|so-called]] crafty brāhmaṇas would have been effective. Writers, Western or Indian, that, relying on the notions current in the 19th and 20th centuries, pass severe and unmeasured strictures on the provisions for [[brahmanas|brāhmaṇas]] in the Purāṇas do great injustice to the authors of Purāṇas that flourished about a thousand years ago or more. Such writers should compare Indian medieval conditions, ideas and doings of [[brahmanas|brāhmaṇas]] with the claims of the Popes, Christian priests, Inquisitions and the state of monastic Orders in Europe from about the 10th century A. D. to the 15th century. In comparison it would be [[P933]] [[‘found|found]] that the conditions in Europe were terribly worse than in India in those [[conturier.|centuries.]]49

As a consequence of the abovementioned dominating [[prinoiple|principle]] the Purāṇas strongly recommend dāna (gifts, [[parti oularly|particularly]] of food), pilgrimages and baths in sacred waters, vratas, [[ahimsa,|ahiṁsā,]] bhakti, repetition of the name of god, śrāddhas &c. These will be briefly illustrated below.

The Purāṇas institute a comparison between solemn Vedic [[Haorifices|sacrifices]] on the one hand and pilgrimages and baths at sacred places on the other. The Vanaparva50 contrasts the two [[29|as]] follows, [["|“the]] solemn [[saorificos|sacrifices]] promulgated by the sages cannot be accomplished by a poor man; sacrifices require many [[imple. ments|implements]] and a collection of various materials, which are secured by kings or sometimes by rich men, but not by poor men who have to rely on themselves alone, who have no helpers and who do not possess means. Going to holy places [[conters|confers]] merit and [[Burpasses|surpasses]] sacrifices. One does not secure that reward by such sacrifices as Agniṣṭoma, in which profuse fees are distributed, which is secured by repairing to sacred [[places.|places.”]] Vide H. of Dh. [[vol, IV|vol. IV]] pp. 561–564 for further encomiums of sacred places and the virtues to be cultivated for reaping the full merit of [[pilgrimages,|pilgrimages.]]

In pursuance of the same dominant principle the [[Anusa. sana-parva|Anuśāsana-parva]] and Purāṇas extol fasts and [[vṛatas.|vratas.]] The [[Anuśādang|Anuśāsana-]]parva51 [[P934]] [[(127. 5-6 )|(107. 5-6)]] remarks that fasts are equal to sacrifices in the matter of rewards. They are put forward as superior to sacrifices in Padma.52 It says: Viṣṇuvrata is super-eminent; a hundred Vedic sacrifices are not equal to it; by performing a [[Bacrifice|sacrifice]] one may go to heaven, but one who observes the [[Kārti kavrata|Kārtikavrata]] goes to [[Vaikuntha|Vaikuṇṭha]] (the world of Viṣṇu). For the exaggerated importance attached to fasts and vratas, vide pp. 43-45 above.

First dāna. High eulogies of gifts have been sung from the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] downwards. The subject of dāna (gifts) in all its aspects has been dealt with in the H. of Dh. vol. [[II,|II.]] pp. [[837-888,|837–888.]] The Mahābhārata in numerous places (particularly in the whole of Anuśāsana-parva) and the Purāṇas such as Matsya chap. [[82 92|82–92]] and 274–289, Agni chap. 208–213, Varāha 99-111, Padma V. 21. 81-213 (which agree almost verbatim with Matsya 83-92), Padma [[II,|II.]] 39-40 and 94, [[III,|III.]] 24, Kūrma II. 26 devote a great deal of space to the subject of [[dānns.|dānas.]] But here only two topics in relation to gifts will be dealt with, viz. [[.gifts|gifts]] of food and gifts to brāhmaṇas. The [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] condemns the person as merely a sinful one who does not offer food to the gods nor to his friends and uses it only to fill his own [[belly,|belly.]]53 The Ait. Br. and the Tai.54 [[Br,|Br.]] speak of [[food’|‘food’]] [[asprāna’(life).|as ‘prāṇa’ (life).]] The Baud.55 Dh. S. [[states” all|states “all]] beings depend on food, the Veda says ‘food is life,’ therefore food should be given [[to|(to]] others), food is the highest offering”. Manu56 and Viṣṇu Dh. S. [[state the|state ’the]] man who cooks food only for his own sake (and not for offering to gods and [[mon)|men)]] eats merely sin’. The [[Bhagavadgitā|Bhagavadgītā]] carries the same [[message those|message ’those]] who eat food [[loft|left]] after offering in sacrifice are [[frood|freed]] from all [[sids,|sins,]] but those who cook food for their sake alone eat sin’. The Padma has a fine passage ’those who always feed the cripple, the blind, children, old men, persons that are [[dis ogsed,|diseased,]] [[P935]] helpless and pinched by penury, always enjoy bliss in heaven; there is no end to the merit (accumulated) by [[con structing|constructing]] wells and tanks, where [[aquatio|aquatic]] animals and others moving on land drink water when they desire, for water is the life of living beings and life is centred in [[water.|water’.]] The [[distribu tion|distribution]] of food particularly to learned brāhmaṇas is highly praised in [[Brahma-parāpa|Brahma-purāṇa]]57 218. 10-32, Padma V. 19. 289-307m, [[agai|Agni]] 211. 44-46. [[The|‘The]] gift of food is superior to all gifts; food is the [[Tife|life]] of men, from food spring all beings; the worlds are [[depen dent|dependent]] on food, therefore food is praised; man secures heaven by the gift of food; a person who joyfully gives food acquired by just means to brāhmaṇas deeply learned in the Veda [[ia|is]] released from all [[sids’|sins’]] says Brahma. The Agnipurāṇa says [[“The|‘The]] gifts of elephants, horses, chariots, male and female slaves and houses do not come up even to a sixteenth part of the gift of food (in merit); a person who committing [[&|a]] great sin afterwards distributes food becomes free from all sins and secures [[everlast ing|everlasting]] worlds’ (211. 44-46). The Kūrma58 [[prescribes a|prescribes ‘a]] man should give to a [[brahmavārin|brahmacārin]] (Vedic student) food every day (i. e. when he comes begging); thereby he becomes free from all sins and reaches the abode of [[Brahma’.|Brahmā’.]] Similarly, Padma [[oalls|calls]] upon house-holders to give as much cooked food to ascetics as would fill the begging [[bowl|bowl.]] From very ancient times a [[house holder|householder]] was called upon to perform five Yajñas [[( sacrifices)|(sacrifices)]] daily, two of which were [[Balıharana|Baliharaṇa]] and honouring a guest ([[Mazu III,|Manu III.]] 70) and he was to place food on the ground for persons who had lost caste, who suffered from loathsome diseases, to [[dāṇdālas,|cāṇḍālas,]] dogs, crows and even insects59 (Āp. Dh. S. II. 4. [[9.5,|9. 5,]] [[Mam|Manu]] III. 92). Vide H. of Dh. vol. [[II,|II.]] pp. 745-747 on [[Vaiśvadoya|Vaiśvadeva]] and [[Baliharana.|Baliharaṇa.]] Underlying these provisions was the noble [[senti ment|sentiment]] of universal kindliness and charity, the persistent [[ides|idea]] that, in spite of social gradations, rules and prejudices dividing [[P936]] men from each other, one Supreme Light [[poryades|pervades]] and [[illumi. nates|illuminates]] the meanest of creatures and makes the whole world kin. This spirit of a householder to regard it as his duty to [[offor|offer]] food to all needy persons and particularly to poor and deserving students and brāhmaṇas has prevailed almost to the present day, though during the last few years shortage of food, high prices and rationing have undermined it a great deal.

Then as to gifts to [[brāhmanas.|brāhmaṇas.]] In the first place, it must be remembered that all [[brāhmapas|brāhmaṇas]] were not priests and are not priests in modern [[timos.|times.]] Similarly, all priests in all Indian temples and shrines are not brāhmaṇas. Temple priests are comparatively a later institution and in olden times they were looked down upon and are regarded as inferior brāhmaṇas [[oven|even]] in modern times. Manu (III. 152) states that a devalaka (a brāhmaṇa who performed for remuneration service before the image in a temple) was unfit to be invited at a śrāddha or to officiate in a sacrifice to gods, if he continuously served for three years in that capacity. The ideal set before brāhmaṇas from [[Yery|very]] ancient times was of poverty, of plain living and high thinking, of abandoning the active pursuit of riches, of devotion to the study of the Veda and śāstras, of cherishing a high culture and of handing down literature and cultural outlook. [[Smrtis|Smṛtis]] like Yāj. [[I,|I.]] 213 recommend that even if a [[brābmaṇa|brāhmaṇa]] be fit for receiving a religious gift he should refuse gifts and thereby [[he-secures|he secures]] the same worlds that habitual donors secure. It was for preserving such high ideals among brāhmaṇas that [[Yaj.|Yāj.]] I. 333 prescribes that a king should make gifts of cows, gold and land and should bestow on learned brāhmaṇas houses and requisites for marriage (maidens, expenses of marriages &c.). Vide H. of Dh. [[VOL. II. Pp.|vol. II. pp.]] 856-858 where references are given to inscriptions containing donations to [[brābmanas|brāhmaṇas]] of houses and marriage expenses. In these days every body talks of the high culture and literary traditions of ancient India. But who cultivated the vast Vedic and other Sanskrit literature, [[presoryad|preserved]] it and propagated it for several millenia? The answer would have to be that it was mostly due to some of the [[brahınanas|brāhmaṇas]] who stuck to the ancient ideal for thousands of years. If the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] can be put forward in these days as the most ancient of the literary monuments in any Aryan language, who preserved its more than ten thousand verses with unparallelled care by oral transmission without hardly any variant readings. The reply will again have to be that the [[brābmanas|brāhmaṇas]] did it by a [[P937]] self-denying ordinance. The [[Brābmanas|Brāhmaṇas]] had to study the Veda with auxiliary lores without60 an eye to any ulterior motive, but simply as a duty and to understand the meaning thereof, they were to teach the Veda and other lores without demanding beforehand a fee, they had to bring up their own families, [[per form|perform]] sacrifices and make gifts themselves. The only substantial [[Bources|sources]] of income were officiating at sacrifices and religious rites and receiving gifts. These sources must always have been variable, fitful and precarious. The brāhmaṇas had no power to tax people as in the case of tithes in the West. Nor had they a regularly paid hierarchy of deacons, priests, bishops and archbishops as in the Anglican [[Church|Church.]] Therefore, the [[brahmanas|brāhmaṇas]] are advised to approach the king or a rich person for their livelihood (vide Gautama [[IX.63|IX. 63]] ‘Yogakṣemārtham-[[Isvaram|īśvaram]] adhigaccet). It should be noted that before Buddhism spread far and wide the sūtras and [[smstis|smṛtis]] emphasized that religious gifts should be made only to a worthy [[brāhmana,|brāhmaṇa,]] learned and well-conducted. For example, Āpastamba61 prescribes ‘one should invite for dinner in all religious acts brāhmaṇas that are pure, that have studied the Veda and that one should distribute gifts at a proper time and place, [[ori|on]] occasions of purificatory rites and when there is a worthy recipient.’ To the same effect are Vas. Dh. S. III. 8, VI. 30, Manu (III. 128, 132, [[IV,|IV.]] 31), [[Yaj.|Yāj.]] [[(1.|(I.]] 201), [[Daksa (IL.|Dakṣa (III.]] 26 and 31). Not every brāhmaṇa was in those ancient times a proper recipient for a gift, but he had to possess qualities of what is called ‘pātra’. A few definitions of pātra may with advantage be cited here. The [[Anugāgar parva|Anuśāsana-parva]]62 has a long passage emphasizing the qualities of [[deserving|a deserving]] brāhmaṇa : ‘gifts made to good brāhmaṇas that are free from anger, that are intent on dharma, are devoted to truth and [[self control|self-control]] [[P938]] yield great rewards; the sages regard that [[brahmana|brāhmaṇa]] as pātra who studies the four Vedas with their [[argas|aṅgas]] (auxiliary lores like phonetics, grammar [[&o.),|&c.),]] who is active in doing six acts [[(viz,|(viz.]] abstaining from wine and meat, observing the bounds of morality, purity, study of the Veda, sacrifices, making gifts). Even a single eminent brāhmaṇa endowed with intellect, Vedic learning, good [[conduot|conduct]] and character saves the whole family; one should bring from afar a brāhmaṇa on hearing that he is possessed of good qualities and is approved by good men and should welcome him and honour him in all ways’. [[Yaj. furni shes|Yāj. furnishes]]63 a brief but striking definition of a worthy [[brāhmaṇa not|brāhmaṇa ’not]] by Vedic learning alone nor by tapas (austere life) alone worthiness arises; that person is declared to be pātra (worthy recipient for a religious gift) where both these (i. e. Vedic learning and tapas) and good conduct [[exist.|exist’.]] Manu provides that religious gifts given to a brāhmaṇa who has not studied the Veda or who is avaricious or deceitful are [[fruitlegs|fruitless]] and lead the donor to hell (IV. 192-194). The [[Bhagavadgitā|Bhagavadgītā]] also (17.22) condemns [[&|a]] gift given to an unworthy person as tāmasa [[( affected|(affected]] by tamas, arising from ignorance or delusion).

As Buddhism [[grow|grew]] in popularity and secured [[algo|also]] royal support, the brāhmaṇas had to tackle several tasks. They had to keep the number of brāhmaṇas at a high level, they had to find maintenance for those devoted to the deep study of the Veda and they had to make accommodation with [[provalent|prevalent]] Buddhist thoughts by assimilating as many of them as possible in their [[owa|own]] writings. Every brāhmaṇa could not possess the memory, the intelligence and the persistent endeavour for long years required for memorizing and mastering even his own [[Voda|Veda]] and its subsidiary Literature. If one [[bundred|hundred]] brāhmaṇa families were patronized, hardly ten percent of them could have been masters of their own Veda, but there was always the possibility that those who were not themselves good Vedic scholars might have sons, some of whom might turn out to be profound students of the [[Voda.|Veda.]] Therefore, the number of [[brāhmapas|brāhmaṇas]] was to be increased and they had to be fed and not to be allowed to fritter away energy and time in working for their bread. It is mainly due to these factors that some of the [[Purīnas|Purāṇas]] contain [[incassant end|incessant and]] frantic appeals for gifts to [[brahmanas,|brāhmaṇas.]]

[[P939]] At the time when most of the Purāṇas were composed, the brāhmaṇas were hemmed in by great [[dificulties|difficulties]] and hostile forces. From about the 3rd century [[B.O.|B. C.]] to the 7th century A. D. Buddhism enjoyed royal patronage under such great kings as [[Asoka, Kaniska and Harga.|Aśoka, Kaniṣka and Harṣa.]] Buddhism was not really a revolt against caste, but against the sacrificial system, against the Veda and its authority to show the way to [[salvation,|salvation.]] Buddha did not found a new religion, but he was a great [[re former|reformer]] of Hinduism. He laid sole stress on moral effort, [[ahinsa,|ahiṁsā,]] satya &c. which had already been integrated into Hinduism and were part of it and which continue to this day to be part of Hinduism. Buddha in his first sermon in the deer-park near Banaras preached that one who renounced the world should shun two extremes64 viz. the pursuit of pleasures and the practice of useless austerities, that it is the course discovered by him that led to wisdom and [[nirvāna.|nirvāṇa.]] He expounded the four noble Axioms or Truths [[(Aryasatyāni)|(Āryasatyāni)]] viz. suffering [[( duḥkha),|(duḥkha),]] the cause of suffering65 viz. [[tanha i.e. traṇā|taṇhā i. e. tṛṣṇā]] (duḥkha-samudaya), the suppression of suffering [[( duḥkha-nirodha),|(duḥkha-nirodha),]] and the way that leads to the suppression of suffering [[(duḥkha-nirodhagamini patipada).|(duḥkha-nirodhagāminī paṭipadā).]] This last is the Noble Eight-fold path ([[agtangiko|aṣṭāṅgiko]]66 [[P940]] mārgaḥ) viz. right views, right thoughts or aspirations, right speech, right actions, right living, right exertion, right [[recollec tion|recollection]] (or mindfulness), right meditation [[(i.e.|(i. e.]] briefly leading a virtuous life). These doctrines preached to all by the noble Buddha and his disciples were attractive as remarked by Rhys [[Davida,|Davids,]]67 particularly to the śūdras whose social position was low in the Vedic times and also in the days of the smṛtis. No one could study the Veda in the presence of a śūdra, a [[sūdra|śūdra]] could not [[parform|perform]] sacrifices and had to serve the three higher varṇas in very ancient times. Almost the same position was assigned to him by Manu (VIII. 413) viz. that he was created by God for serving the [[brāhmana,|brāhmaṇa,]] though it is doubtful whether this could be or was enforced. Vide H. of [[Dn.|Dh.]] vol. II, pp. 33-36 and pp. 154-164 for the position and disabilities of śūdras from the Vedic times up to the days of the [[Smortis.|Smṛtis.]] It should not, however, be supposed that all India had become Buddhist. Millions still remained Hindus. There was the danger and fear that with royal [[patron age|patronage]] and the attractive features of Buddha’s teachings large masses might forsake their ancient faith.

The brāhmaṇas of the times when Buddhism was at its peak had to strive to keep the banner of the Vedic religion flying, to deprive Buddhism of its [[bold|hold]] on the masses of the people and even on the intelligentsia and to make them stick to the old fold. Buddhism itself had changed a great deal in its ideals, [[dootrinos|doctrines]] sometime before the beginning of the Christian era and for centuries thereafter. Buddha’s original doctrines were aimed at individual effort and salvation of the individual by his own effort and self-culture. Early Buddhist texts deny the existence of anything like a soul68 and find no place for [[P941]] the idea of God. Though Buddha spoke of [[Nirvana|Nirvāṇa]] he did not clearly define it nor does he specify the condition of the [[indivi dual|individual]] when he enters nirvāṇa. Aśvaghoṣa compares [[nirvana|nirvāṇa]] to the extinction of a flame (Saundarananda, chap. [[XVI,|XVI.]] 28-29). [[Bocanse|Because]] the doctrine of karma was deeply rooted in the popular mind at the time of Buddha, Buddhism took over that [[dootrine,|doctrine,]] which, to men who are not Buddhists, appears to be [[contradio tory|contradictory]] of the denial of the existence of a soul. The word ‘Dhamma’ is used in three senses in the Pāli [[Dhammapada’|‘Dhammapada’]] (which being mentioned in the questions of Milinda is earlier than the 2nd century B. C.) viz. (1) the truth or law preached by Buddha, (2) thing or form, (3) way or mode of life.

As stated above, the original Buddhism preached by the Buddha and his followers in the first century or two after his [[parinirvana|parinirvāṇa]] was more or less a strict ethical code for individuals who sought salvation from the misery of the world. The three central conceptions of very early Buddhism [[wore|were]] the three refuges [[(or rainas )|(or ratnas)]] Buddha, Dharma and Sangha, the Four Noble Truths and the Eightfold Path. Gradually a new doctrine was evolved.69 It came to be thought that to care for one’s own deliverance and spend all efforts thereon was rather selfish, that as the Buddha himself out of compassion for suffering humanity worked for forty-five years to lead men to salvation by his exhortations and sermons, so a Buddhist should prefer [[tor|to]] put off his own deliverance, should work for the deliverance of his fellowmen out of compassion [[(karuna)|(karuṇā)]] for their miserable lot and in doing so should be ready to be born again and again, should not care for his own salvation and should not be afraid of [[satnsāra.|saṁsāra.]] Those holding these latter views deified Buddha, taught that Buddhahood was attained by Siddhārtha after [[under going|undergoing]] numerous births in doing service and rendering help to others and that this was a superior code of conduct (Mahāyāna, the Great Vehicle or Way) to the selfish code of salvation for an individual himself [[( which|(which]] came to be called [[Hinayāna,|Hīnayāna,]] the [[leggor|lesser]] Vehicle or Way). This extra-regarding gospel of Mahāyāna was very attractive and won great support in most countries of [[Asia,|Asia.]]70

[[P942]] This [[dootrine|doctrine]] of [[Bodhisattves|Bodhisattvas]] (meaning ‘beings destined to be enlightened’) is not consistent with the gospel of Buddha as [[proached|preached]] by him in his first sermon at Banaras. There is a difference of ideals between [[Hinayāna|Hīnayāna]] and [[Mahāyāna|Mahāyāna.]] The original gospel relies on self-effort and moral regeneration and on the elimination of suffering and misery by the extinction of all passions and of hankerings or desires and of the desire of [[P943]] life71 itself. Buddha’s original teaching regarded it [[ag & Waste|as a waste]] of time to ponder over such questions as ‘Have I existed during the ages that are past or have I not? Shall I exist during the ages of the future? Do I after all exist or am I not?’ The [[Sabbāgavasutta|Sabbāsavasutta]] (9-13) says that a wise man walking in the noble eightfold path understands what things ought to be [[con sidered|considered]] and what things ought not to be considered. Vide [[8.B.E. VOL XI,|S.B.E. vol. XI.]] pp. 298-300. Buddhism brought half of Asia under [[ito|its]] influence not only by its promise of salvation to all by self-help, but more so by its teachings of profound tenderness, of active charity, of goodness and [[gentlenesa.|gentleness.]] Mahāyāna laid great stress on doing good to all and on bhakti. Both the original teaching of [[Hinayāna|Hīnayāna]] and the [[Mabāyāna|Mahāyāna]] teaching are attractive in their own way.

Buddhism insisted on the five [[silas|śīlas]]72 that were binding on all Buddhists viz. prohibition of injury to and [[destruotion|destruction]] of life, of theft, of sexual impurity, of lying and of [[intoxioating|intoxicating]] liquors. Five more precepts [[( which|(which]] together with the preceding five were called [[Daśaśiksāpadas)|Daśaśikṣāpadas)]] were added for Buddhist priests, viz. prohibition of eating at forbidden hours, of attending [[wordly|worldly]] amusements such as dancing, song, music and shows, of the use of unguents and ornaments, of the use of a large or ornamented couch and of the receiving of gold and silver. About the [[silas|śīlas]] it is clear that they were adopted from the ancient [[Upaniṣadio|Upaniṣadic]] and Dharmasūtra teachings. The [[Chandogya|Chāndogya]] narrates how Aśvapati, king of Kekaya, boasted before five great householders and [[theologiang’in|theologians ‘in]] my73 kingdom there [[ia|is]] no thief, no miser, no drunkard, no man without a [[firs-altar|fire-altar]] in his house, [[Do|no]] ignorant person, no adulterer, much less an adulteress’. The same Upaniṣad74 quotes an ancient [[verse a|verse ‘a]] man who steals gold, who drinks liquor, who dishonours his guru’s bed, who kills [[& brahmapathese|a brāhmaṇa—these]] four fall and a fifth also that associates with any one of [[those|these]] four.’ It will be shown [[P944]] a little later how [[ahingā wag|ahiṁsā was]] emphasized even in the Upaniṣads. Thus [[ahimsa,|ahiṁsā,]] non-stealing, sexual purity, truthfulness had already been emphasized in the oldest [[Upanisads.|Upaniṣads.]] That an [[agoetic|ascetic]]75 had to give up all property and beg for keeping body and soul together is made clear by [[Br.|Bṛ.]] Up. III. [[5.1 and IV.4.22,|5. 1 and IV. 4. 22,]] [[Jabalopanisad 5.|Jābālopaniṣad 5,]] Gautama III. 10-13, [[Vasistha x.|Vasiṣṭha X.]] The other five precepts for priests such as not receiving gold or silver or giving up unguents, flowers, dancing, singing and music are laid down by Gautama76 II. 19 and III. 4, [[Vasisthax.6&c.|Vasiṣṭha X. 6 &c.]] for Vedic students and ascetics. Vide H. Kern (in ‘Manual of Indian Buddhism, Grundriss p. 70) who remarks that the superior morality for monks is nothing else but the rule of life for the [[dirija|dvija]] in the 4th [[ūśrama,|āśrama,]] when he is a yati and all the details were taken from the Dharma-sūtras and [[Dharmaśāstras,|Dharmaśāstras.]]

[[Ahimsā – The|Ahiṁsā—The]] Mahābhārata and the [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] lay great emphasis on [[abiinsā|ahiṁsā]] (not harming or giving pain to a living creature). The [[Upanisads|Upaniṣads]] too emphasized [[ahimsā.|ahiṁsā.]] The [[Chandogya|Chāndogya]]77 does so in several passages. In III. [[17.4|17. 4]] it says ’tapas, charity, straight-forwardness, [[ahimsa,|ahiṁsā,]] speaking the truth-these are the fees of this (sacrifice without ceremonial)’. While describing how the person who has attained true [[know ledge|knowledge]] of the Self does not return [[( to this sainsāra),|(to this saṁsāra),]] the [[Chandogya|Chāndogya]] says that [[he|‘he]] causes no pain to any creature except at [[tirthas’.|tīrthas’.]] The [[Br.|Bṛ.]] Up. [[(V.2.)|(V. 2.)]] says how [[Prajapati|Prajāpati]] told the gods, the asuras and men that the sound ‘da da da’ produced by thundering [[P945]] clouds conveys to the gods the necessity of self-restraint [[( dama),|(dama),]] to [[theasures|the asuras]] of compassion [[(daya)|(dayā)]] and to men of charity [[(dana).|(dāna).]] Gautama78 specifies eight virtues of the soul, the first of which is compassion for all beings and states that he who has the forty [[saīskāras|saṁskāras]] performed on him but does not [[possong|possess]] the eight virtues does not secure absorption into [[brahman|brahma.]] The [[Adiparya|Ādiparva]] [[provides abimsā|provides ‘ahiṁsā]] is the highest dharma for all beings; therefore a [[brahmana|brāhmaṇa]] should never harm (or give pain [[to )|to)]] any being’. The words ‘[[ahirsā|ahiṁsā]] paramo dharmaḥ’ ([[ahimsā|ahiṁsā]] is the highest dharma) [[ocour|occur]] very frequently in the Mahābhārata79 (e. g. in [[Dropaparva|Droṇaparva]] 192. 38, [[Santi|Śānti]] [[265.6,|265. 6,]] [[339.|329.]] 18, [[Anusasana 115,25,|Anuśāsana 115. 25,]] 116. 38, [[Aivamedhikaparva|Āśvamedhikaparva]] 28. 16-18, 43. 21). [[Santiparva|Śāntiparva]] [[(296.22-24)|(296. 22-24)]] enumerates thirteen virtues common to all men, of which freedom from cruelty and [[ahirsa|ahiṁsā]] are the first two. [[Vasistha|Vasiṣṭha]] [[IV.4,|IV. 4,]] Manu [[x.|X.]] 63, [[Yaj.|Yāj.]] I. 122 prescribe certain virtues as necessary in men of all varṇas. Vide [[.|H.]] of Dh. vol. II. p. 10 notes 25-27.

A few examples of emphasis on [[ahimsā|ahiṁsā]] in the [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] may be cited here. The [[Vamanapurana1534|Vāmanapurāṇa]]80 provides : [[ahinsa,|ahiṁsā,]] [[truth fulness,|truthfulness,]] [[P946]] absence of stealing, charity, forbearance, [[self-oontrol,|self-control,]] quiescence of senses, absence of poor spirit or weakness, purity, tapas-this is the tenfold dharma [[spplicable|applicable]] to all varṇas. The Padma says : [[Dot|not]] by [[(the study of )|(the study of)]] the Vedas, nor by [[gifte|gifts]] nor by tapas, nor by sacrifices do men who kill creatures reach the goal of heaven; [[ahimsa|ahiṁsā]] is the highest dharma, the highest tapas and the highest charity–this is what the [[søges|sages]] always say; [[mon|men]] that are compassionate treat [[fios,|flies,]] reptiles, stinging insects, lice and the like and human beings as [[themselves,|themselves.]] Matsya goes so far as to [[state ;|state;]] great sages do not commend [[sacrifios|sacrifice]] in which there is killing; by donating grains of corn gathered in a field, roots, fruits, vegetables, vessel for carrying water according to their ability, sages practising [[austorities|austerities]] became established in heaven; absence of hatred and greed, self-restraint, compassion towards all beings, control of senses, celibacy, tapas, tenderness, [[farbearance|forbearance]] and firmness—this is the root of the ancient [[dharua,|dharma,]] which is difficult to accomplish. The [[Brahmānda|Brahmāṇḍa]] (II. 31. 35 [[tagmād-ahimsa|‘tasmād-ahiṁsā]] dharmasya dvāram-uktam [[maharṣibhiḥ’ )|maharṣibhiḥ’)]] says that great sages have declared that [[ahimsā|ahiṁsā]] is the door of dharma. The Padma (V. 43. [[38 )|38)]] says [[there|‘there]] is no dāna nor tapas equal to [[ahimsā’.|ahiṁsā’.]] It is interesting to note that the [[Mateya|Matsya]] and [[Brabmāṇda|Brahmāṇḍa]] regard [[ahimsā|ahiṁsā]] as ‘sanātana dharma’ and condemn animal sacrifices. The [[Kurma|Kūrma]]81 provides ‘[[ahirai,|ahiṁsā,]] truthfulness, non-stealing, celibacy and non-possession of [[wealth these|wealth—these]] are briefly declared yamas that produce purity of mind among [[mon,|men.]] The great [[sagos deolare|sages declare]] that [[ahinsa|ahiṁsā]] consists in [[cansing|causing]] no pain to all [[boings|beings]] at all times by thought, word or [[P947]] [[dood.|deed.]] There is no dharma superior to [[ahimah,|ahiṁsā,]] no happiness higher than [[the praotice of )|(the practice of)]] [[ahimsā;|ahiṁsā;]] the injury [[(to life )|(to life)]] that [[ia|is]] caused [[socording|according]] to (Vedic) [[pracepts|precepts]] is declared to be [[ahirnar.’|ahiṁsā.’]] The Upaniṣads commended a qualified [[abimsi,|ahiṁsā,]] while the original Pali books like the [[Sāmāñña-phala-gutta|Sāmañña-phala-sutta]] forbad injury to all living beings. Most of the [[Puranas,|Purāṇas,]] in order to convince the masses that they did not lag behind the Buddhist preachings, generally insist upon unqualified [[ahimsa.|ahiṁsā.]] Time brings about strange changes. Professing Buddhists in Ceylon, China, Japan and many other countries have no objection to partaking of fish and meat, while following the insistent advice of the [[Purinas,|Purāṇas,]] millions of Indian people (not only brāhmaṇas but also others like [[vaisyas|vaiśyas]] and [[sūdras|śūdras]] if Vaiṣṇavas) have been [[striot vege tarians|strict vegetarians]] for centuries, though Buddhism vanished from India centuries ago.

It may be noted, however, that some of the [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] are against [[varrying|carrying]] the doctrine of [[ahimsa|ahiṁsā]] to extremes. The [[Brahmapda|Brahmāṇḍa]] and [[Vayu|Vāyu]] both say that there is no sin, great or small, in killing a person (e. g. a tyrant or a [[desperado ),|desperado),]] when many will live in happiness by his [[death,|death.]]82

[[Parta-The|Pūrta—The]] Purāṇas lay the greatest emphasis on what is called [[purtadharma,|pūrtadharma,]] works of public utility, charity, [[soola!|social]] service and the relief of the poor and distressed. The word [[Istāpūrta|Iṣṭāpūrta]] occurs in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] once83 [[May|‘May]] you be united in the highest heaven with your [[pitys ( ancestors),|pitṛs (ancestors),]] with [[Yams|Yama]] and with [[iṣtāpārta’( merit|iṣṭāpūrta’ (merit]] acquired by sacrifices and works of public [[utility ).|utility).]] The word [[ista|iṣṭa]] occurs several times in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] (I. [[162, 16|162. 15,]] [[1.|I.]] 164. 15, X. 11. [[%,|2,]] [[X82.2|X. 82. 2]]), but the meaning is not certain except in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 11. 2 where it appears to mean [[sacrifice’.|‘sacrifice’.]] [[Purta|Pūrta]] also occurs in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] VI. 16. 18 and [[VIII,|VIII.]] 46. 21, but the meaning is not certain. [[“Iṣtāpūrta’|‘Iṣṭāpūrta’]] occurs in several [[Upanigades|Upaniṣads.]]

[[P948]] The [[Chandogya|Chāndogya]] states84 [[But|‘But]] they who, living in a village, practise [[( a life of ) sacrifions,|(a life of) sacrifices,]] works of public utility and [[alms giving|alms-giving]] reach toward smoke &c.’. Similarly, the Praśna-upaniṣad [[Aggerts|asserts]] ’those, who practise the (mode of) sacrifices and works of [[publio|public]] utility as [[aotivities|activities]] to be engaged in, reach only the world of the Moon, and it is these that again return to this world’. The [[Mundaka|Muṇḍaka]] says ‘deluded people regarding sacrifices and works of public utility as the best do not know [[( recognize)|(recognize)]] any other higher good; having enjoyed [[( their|(their]] reward) on the top of heaven, they again enter this world or even a lower one’.85 Manu speaks of ‘[[iṣta’|iṣṭa’]] and ‘pūrta’ and recommends that one should always practise with a pleased heart sacrificial gifts and gifts of the pūrta kind according to one’s ability on securing a deserving [[brābmapa.|brāhmaṇa.]] The Amarakośa defines [[“iṣta’|‘iṣṭa’]] as sacrifices and [[pārta’|‘pūrta’]] as works such as digging a well or tank. The [[Mārkandeya|Mārkaṇḍeya]]86 defines them as follows ‘Maintaining the sacred [[firos,|fires,]] tapas, truthfulness, [[study.|study]] of the [[Voda,|Veda,]] [[hogpitality|hospitality]] and [[Vaiśvadova-these|Vaiśvadeva-these]] are called [[ista;|iṣṭa;]] digging wells and tanks and building temples and distribution of food to those that need [[it these|it—these]] are declared to be [[purta.|pūrta.]] The Agnipurāṇa has similar [[vorses.|verses.]] The Padma (VI. 243. 10–14) cites the following as [[dharmakarya|dharmakārya]] (religious works); temples of Viṣṇu and Śiva, tanks, wells, lotus ponds, forest of [[vata,|vaṭa,]] pippala, mango, kakkola, jambu and nimba trees, flower garden, distribution of food from [[mording|morning]] to [[Sunset,|sunset,]] water distribution outside towns [[&o.|&c.]] The Skanda87 says: the term ‘pūrta’ is applied in the [[charma sūstras|dharma-śāstras]] to the erection of temples, construction of tanks, ponds and wells, laying out parks. Padma ([[VI, 244, 34-35 )|VI. 244. 34-35)]] says that those who build monasteries, cow stables, houses of rest on roads, [[P949]] dwellings for [[ascetios,|ascetics,]] cottages for the poor and [[helplegg,|helpless,]] extensive house for Veda study, houses for [[brahmanas,|brāhmaṇas,]] enter the world of Indra [[(i. o.|(i. e.]] heaven). Atri [[sayg|says]]88 that [[Iṣta|Iṣṭa]] and [[Parts|Pūrta]] are dharma common to all dvijas; a [[sūdra|śūdra]] is entitled to [[performi|perform]] [[purta-dharmas|pūrta-dharmas]] but not Vaidika rites [[(I. e.|(i. e.]] sacrifices). The [[Anusasana-parva (ohap.|Anuśāsana-parva (chap.]] 58) describes how parks should be [[lald|laid]] out and tanks constructed with trees on their banks. But the [[Varihapurāṇa|Varāhapurāṇa]] and some [[smrtis|smṛtis]] [[wont eo|went so]] far [[and|as]] to declare that a man secures only heaven by [[iṣta,|iṣṭa,]] but he secures [[moksa|mokṣa]] (final release from [[samsāra )|saṁsāra)]] by pūrta89.

Sometimes the Purāṇas express ideas that might strike us as rather modern, when they put forward social service and removal of suffering and distress as the highest dharma. In the [[Mārkandeya|Mārkaṇḍeya]]90 a king solemnly states ‘men do not obtain that happiness in heaven or in the world of [[Brahma,|Brahmā,]] which [[springe|springs]] from giving relief to distressed beings. Sacrifices, gifts, tapas do not conduce to the relief here and in the next world of that man whose heart is not set on relieving the distressed’. The Viṣṇu [[recommends &|recommends ‘a]] wise man should say [[(and do )|(and do)]] that alone by thought, word and deed which would be for the benefit of creatures here and hereafter’. The [[Skanda-purana (Kasikhanda) aver|Skanda-purāṇa (Kāśīkhaṇḍa) avers]] ‘adversities of those good men in whose heart doing good to others is awake [[(i.e.aotive)|(i. e. active)]] vanish and prosperity comes to them at every step. That purity is not secured by [[bathe|baths]] at holy places, that reward is not obtained by numerous gifts, that (result) is not obtained by severe austerities, that is obtained by doing good to others. After churning all extensive [[diota tha|dicta the]] conclusion reached is this viz. there is no dharma higher than doing good to others and there is no sin greater than [[barmning|harming]] [[P950]] others. The Brahma [[states the|states ’the]] life of the man who always strives for the good of others is fruitful (blessed); fire, water, the Sun, the earth and various kinds of crops exist for the benefit of others, and particularly the good [[( exist|(exist]] for others’ [[benefit).|benefit)’.]]

It is surprising that the [[Bhagavata|Bhāgavata]] avers what is in advance of modern [[socialistio|socialistic]] doctrines ‘men have ownership over only that much as would fill their belly; he who thinks as his own what is more than that is a thief and deserves punishment [[(As|(as]] such).91

[[Bhaldi-The Puranas|Bhakti—The Purāṇas]] lay very great emphasis on bhakti (religion of loving faith in God). This is not the place to dilate on the history of the cult of Bhakti from the earliest times to modern days. For that purpose there are special treatises [[( some|(some]] of which are noted below) that may be consulted. But a few words on bhakti in general may be said here before going into the [[ques tion|question]] as to what the [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] have to say thereon. Traces of the doctrine of bhakti may be discovered even in the [[Rgvedio|Ṛgvedic]] hymns and mantras, some of which are full of loving faith in God, particularly in some of the hymns and [[vorges|verses]] addressed to [[Varuna|Varuṇa]] and also to Indra. A few examples may be [[cited. All|cited. ‘All]] my thoughts92 (or hymns) praise Indra in unison, seeking light, longing for him, as [[wivos|wives]] embrace their husband, their [[fat|fair]] young lover, they (thoughts) embrace him (Indra), the divine giver of gifts’; ‘your friendship93 (with your devotee) is indestructible [[( everlasting);|(everlasting);]] to him who desires a cow, you become a cow, to him who longs for a horse, may you be a [[horso;"|horse;’]] ‘O Indra, you are far better (or richer) than my father or my brother who does not feed me; (you) and my mother, O Vasu, are equal and protect (me) for (conferring) riches and [[favoura’;|favours’;]] [[“You|‘You]] gave to [[Kaksivat,|Kakṣīvat,]] who offered a hymn and Soma libation to you and who had grown old, [[Vrcaya,|Vṛcayā,]] who was a young [[P951]] woman; you became the wife of [[Vrsañaśva;|Vṛṣaṇaśva;]] all these [[(tavours)|(favours)]] of yours deserve to be loudly proclaimed during the offerings of soma libations’; ‘you who, shining brilliantly, come to each house assuming the form of a small man, [[( Indral)|(O Indra!)]] drink this Soma juice (produced) by being crushed with my teeth and mixed with fried grains, gruel, cake and laud’. [[Comparo Rs.|Compare Ṛg.]] III. 43. 4, [[X|X.]] 42. 11, X. 112. 10 (in all of which Indra is called ‘sakhā’ [[friend )|friend)]] and I. [[104.9,|104. 9,]] [[VII, 32.26|VII. 32. 26]] (in both Indra is said to be like a father).
