It will be clear from these passages that the Vedic [[seges|sages]] had reached the stage of sakhya-bhakti, that the sages believed that Indra was like a mother, that he assumed the form of a wife for the sake of a devotee, that Indra partook soma juice from a devotee who, in the absence of the proper [[imple ments|implements]] for crushing soma stalks, extracted soma juice from soma stalks crushed with the devotee’s own [[teeth,|teeth.]] These stories in the far-off ages of the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] remind us of the stories in the works of the medieval ages about [[Rama|Rāma]] having accepted [[badars|badara]] fruit from a [[Sabari|Śabarī]] (Bhil woman) devotee who first tasted them with her teeth to see whether they were [[sweat|sweet]] or sour and the story of the God [[Vithobā|Viṭhobā]] of Pandharpur, having assumed the form of [[& mahar|a mahār]] (an untouchable) and paid up to the [[Moslom|Moslem]] king of Bijapur the money equivalent to the price of corn which, [[Damaji|Dāmājī]] (a great devotee), who was in charge of the royal granary, allowed people distressed by famine to take away. Some mantras addressed to Varuṇa show the same kind of sakhya-bhakti. [[Vasiṣtha|Vasiṣṭha]]94 prays ‘O [[Varupa I|Varuṇa!]] what is that great offence [[foom mitted|(committed]] by me) on account of which you desire to harm me, your friend and bard; declare that to me, O invincible and self-willed God, so that [[( after|(after]] propitiating you) I shall be free from sin and may be able quickly to approach you with [[adoration ‘;|adoration’;]] [[‘whare|‘where]] are those friendships of ours (of you and me) which we safely enjoyed in former times, O self-willed [[Varuṇa?;I|Varuṇa?; I]] (then) went to your big dwelling house that has a thousand doors; whatever offence we mortals commit against the divine [[hosta,|hosts,]] O [[Varuna,|Varuṇa,]] whatever laws of yours we may have violated in our ignorance [[(or heedlessness ),|(or heedlessness),]] do not, O God, harm us on [[80count|account]] of that sin’. It is remarkable that in the [[Rgveda thors|Ṛgveda there]] is a verse in which [[P952]] there is an [[apotheosia|apotheosis]]95 of [[.namas’|‘namas’]] (namaskāra, adoration or [[homage );‘Adoration|homage); ‘Adoration]] itself is mighty, I offer service with [[adora tion;|adoration;]] adoration upholds the heaven and the earth; adoration to the gods, adoration rules these gods, whatever sin is committed [[( by|(by]] me) I worship it away with adoration’.

Though the word ‘bhakti’ does not occur in the principal ancient Upaniṣads, the doctrine of the bhakti schools that it is God’s grace alone that saves the devotee is found in the [[Katha|Kaṭha]] and [[Mundaka Upanisads,|Muṇḍaka Upaniṣads,]] viz. ’this Supreme Soul is not to be attained by expositions (of a teacher) nor by intelligence, nor by much learning; He is to be attained by him alone whom the Supreme Soul favours, to him this Supreme Soul discloses His [[form. “|form.’]]96 This emphasizes the doctrine that God’s Grace alone brings [[balvation|salvation]] to the [[devotee,|devotee.]] The [[Svetāśvataropaniṣad|Śvetāśvataropaniṣad]] employs the word bhakti in the same sense in which it is used in the [[Gita|Gītā]] and97 other works on bhakti. [[These|‘These]] matters declared [[( here)|(here)]] [[roveal|reveal]] themselves to that high-souled person who has the highest faith in God and the same faith in his guru as in God’. The [[sane|same]] [[Upanisad|Upaniṣad]] emphasizes [[&|a]] doctrine of the bhakti school in [[*I,|‘I,]] desirous of [[moksa|mokṣa]] (liberation from [[samsāra ),|saṁsāra),]] surrender myself as my refuge to that God who in former times created (established) Brahmā, who transmitted to him [[(Brahma )|(Brahmā)]] the Vedas, and who illuminates the intellect of the individual [[soul’,|soul’.]]

The word [[prapadye’|‘prapadye’]] in the [[Svetāóvatara|Śvetāśvatara]] serves as the basis of the doctrine of [[prapatti’|‘prapatti’]] in the Vaiṣṇavite system such as that of Rāmānuja.

But among the original sources of the Bhakti cult are the [[Nārā yapiya|Nārāyaṇīya]] section (chapters [[335-351|335–351]] of [[Citraśala|Citraśālā]] ed. = cr. ed. 322–339) of the [[Sāntiparva|Śāntiparva]] and the [[Bhagavadgita.|Bhagavadgītā.]] For the [[P953]] antiquity of [[Krsṇa|Kṛṣṇa]] worship (vide pp. 129-131 above). Megasthenes states that Heracles [[(Harikroṇa ? )|(Harikṛṣṇa?)]] was worshipped by Soursenoi [[(Saurasenas )|(Śaurasenas)]] on the banks of [[Jobaros|Jobares]] (Yamunā) and had two [[oities|cities]] Methora [[(Mathurā )|(Mathurā)]] and Cleisbora [[( Kṭspapura ? ).|(Kṛṣṇapura?).]] In the [[Nārāyaniya|Nārāyaṇīya]] it is stated (in chap. [[335,|335.]] 17-24) that king Uparicara Vasu was a devotee [[( bhakta )|(bhakta)]] of Nārāyaṇa, that he worshipped the Lord of Gods according to the [[sātivata|sāttvata]] rules that were proclaimed by the Sun, that he (Vasu) consecrated his kingdom, wealth, wife and horses to God, thinking that they all belonged to the Bhagavat [[(the Adorable One )|(the Adorable One)]] and performed sacrificial rites according to the Sāttvata rules.

In the [[Sāntiparva, Satvata and Pancarātra|Śāntiparva, Sātvata and Pāñcarātra]] are identified98 and it is said that the seven sages called [[‘citraśikhandin’|‘citraśikhaṇḍin’]] (lit. whose top-knots on the head were bright or wonderful) viz. [[Marici,|Marīci,]] Atri, [[Angiras,|Aṅgiras,]] Pulastya, Pulaha, Kratu and [[Vasistha,|Vasiṣṭha,]] proclaimed the [[(Pāñcarātra )|(Pāñcarātra)]] śāstra and God [[Nārāyana|Nārāyaṇa]] told them that the śāstra would be authoritative in the world and that king Vasu would learn the [[sāstra|śāstra]] from [[Bphaspati|Bṛhaspati]] to whom it would come by degrees from the seven sages. Chapter 336 of [[Santi|Śānti]] declares that to the north of the Milky Sea there was territory called [[Svetadvipa,|Śvetadvīpa,]] where dwelt devotees of [[Nārāyana|Nārāyaṇa]] who were called99 [[• Ekāntin’|‘Ekāntin’]] and Pāñcarātra is called ‘Ekānta-dharma’. [[À|A]] peculiar doctrine of the Pāñcarātra school is that of the four [[wyūhas|vyūhas]] (mūrtis or forms) viz. that the Supreme Person is [[Vasudeva,|Vāsudeva,]] the individual soul is [[Sāṅkarṣana,|Saṅkarṣaṇa,]] [[Pradyuma|Pradyumna]] is mind and springs from [[Saṅkarsana|Saṅkarṣaṇa]] and Aniruddha is [[ahankāra|ahaṅkāra]] and originates from Pradyumna.100 It is this doctrine of the four forms of Vāsudeva, each springing from the preceding, that is refuted according to [[Śhaṅkara|Śaṅkara]] in the Brahmasūtra II. [[%.|2.]] 42-45. The [[śānti|Śānti]] 348. 8 expressly refers to the [[Gitā|Gītā]] as already declared [[P954]] to Arjuna. In101 chap. 349. 64 it is said that [[Saṅkhya,|Sāṅkhya,]] Yoga, [[Panoarātra,|Pāñcarātra,]] Vedas and Pāśupata are five lores that differ in their views and were promulgated by Kapila [[( Saṅkhya ),|(Sāṅkhya),]] [[Hiranya garbha|Hiraṇyagarbha]] (Yoga), Apāntaratamas (the Veda), Śiva [[(Pāśupata ),|(Pāśupata),]] the [[Bhagavat|Bhāgavat]] Himself [[( promulgator|(promulgator]] of Pāñcarātra). The [[Viṣṇu dharmottara|Viṣṇudharmottara]]102 [[remarks for|remarks ‘for]] seeking Brahma (the One Reality in the Universe) there are five Siddhāntas (systems) viz. [[Saṅkhya,|Sāṅkhya,]] Yoga, [[Pāñoarātra,|Pāñcarātra,]]103 Vedas and Pāśupata’. Relying on [[śānti parva|Śāntiparva]] 339. 68 several writers particularly those of the Rāmānuja school assert that the whole of the Pāñcarātra system has [[Vodic|Vedic]] [[P955]] authority, while others like [[Apararka|Aparārka]] p. 13 and [[Paribhayaprakasa|Paribhāṣāprakāśa]] [[(p. 23 )|(p. 23)]] do not accept it as thoroughly [[Vodio|Vedic]] but only [[partialy.|partially.]]

In the Vedāntasūtra104 there are four sūtras dealing with the [[Bhagavata|Bhāgavata]] or [[Panoarātra|Pāñcarātra]] system. The great [[Acāryas|Ācāryas]] are not agreed upon their interpretation, [[Śhaṅkara|Śaṅkara]] saying that all the four sūtras refute some tenets of the [[Bhagavatas,|Bhāgavatas,]] [[Rāmāpnja|Rāmānuja]] saying that the first two sūtras [[( out of four )|(out of four)]] contain refutation of the [[Bhagavata|Bhāgavata]] doctrine and the other two do not. [[Saṅkard cārya|Śaṅkarācārya]] makes it clear that the [[doctrinos|doctrines]] of the [[Bhagavatag|Bhāgavatas]] that the Supreme God Vāsudeva is the Highest Truth, that [[he|He]] assumes four forms, that the worship of Vāsudeva consists in continuous single-minded contemplation of [[Vasudeva|Vāsudeva]] are not the targets for attack, that what is refuted is the [[dootrine|doctrine]] of the Bhāgavatas that the individual soul called [[Saṅkarṣana|Saṅkarṣaṇa]] by them springs from Vāsudeva, that Pradyumna [[( mind)|(mind)]] arises from [[Śhaṅkarasana|Saṅkarṣaṇa]] and that Aniruddha [[(ahamkāra )|(ahaṁkāra)]] arises from Pradyumna. It appears from [[Śhaṅkara’s|Śaṅkara’s]] remarks105 on II. 2. 45 that in his day [[Śāṇdilya|Śāṇḍilya]] was supposed to have promulgated the Bhāgavata or [[Pāncarātra|Pāñcarātra]] śāstra, because he did not find the [[P956]] highest [[blles|bliss]] in all the four Vedas. In the [[Dropaparya|Droṇaparva]] ( 29. 26-29) there is another and different reference to the four [[murtis|mūrtis]] of the Supreme for the benefit of the worlds viz. one performs tapas ([[asuterities|austerities]]) on the earth, the 2nd has an eye on the world’s good and evil deeds; the third comes to the world in a human form and does such acts as men perform; the 4th [[alumbers|slumbers]] for a thousand years and when it arises from slumber [[conferr|confers]] the highest boons on those who deserve them.

It may be noted that even in the [[Mahabharata|Mahābhārata]] [[Nārada’a|Nārada’s]] name is connected with [[Pancarātra.|Pāñcarātra.]] It is said [[’ this|‘this]] very secret doctrine, connected with the four Vedas, having the benefit of [[Saṅkhya|Sāṅkhya]] and Yoga and spoken of as Pañcarātra, was (originally) uttered by the lips of Nārāyaṇa and was again communicated by [[Narada.|Nārada.]]106

Other great sources of the cult of bhakti are the [[Bhagavad gita,|Bhagavadgītā,]] which is expressly mentioned in the [[Nārāyaṇiya|Nārāyaṇīya]] section [[(348.8 %D cr.ed. 336.8),|(348. 8 = cr. ed. 336. 8),]] the [[Bhagavatapurana|Bhāgavatapurāṇa]] and the [[Viṣṇu purāṇa.|Viṣṇupurāṇa.]] The [[Gitā|Gītā]] contains the words bhakti and bhakta several dozens of times. It may be stated here that the so-called [[Narada bhaktisutras,|Nārada-bhaktisūtras,]] [[Narada-pancarātra,|Nārada-pāñcarātra,]] the [[Sandilya-bhakti-sutra|Śāṇḍilya-bhakti-sūtra]] and the several extant [[Palivaratra-samhitas|Pāñcarātra-saṁhitās]] so far published are all later than the [[Gita.|Gītā.]]107 The [[Agnipurana|Agnipurāṇa]] gives the names of 25 works on [[Pancaratra|Pāñcarātra]] quoted in the note below.108 The [[P957]] [[Mahesvara tantra|Maheśvaratantra]] names 25 [[Pancarātra|Pāñcarātra]] tantras promulgated by Viṣṇu, which are condemned therein as having no truth ( 26. 16 ff.).

There is an extensive Literature on the Bhakti cult. Only a few of the important works in Sanskrit, their translations and works in English are noted here. Barth, Hopkins, Keith, Dr. R. G. Bhandarkar and others have propounded different theories as to what [[Krsna|Kṛṣṇa]] represented, how he was identified with Viṣṇu who appears to be another name of the Sun in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] and came to be regarded as the highest God in [[Brahmana|Brāhmaṇa]] times (as in Ait. Br. ‘Agnir-vai devānām [[avamah,|avamaḥ,]] [[Viṣṇuh|Viṣṇuḥ]] [[paramaḥ)|paramaḥ’)]] and came to be identified with sacrifice ([[Yajāo|Yajño]] vai Viṣṇuḥ). When [[Kroṇa,|Kṛṣṇa,]] the friend of the [[Pandavas|Pāṇḍavas]] was identified with the Supreme Spirit, the full-fledged doctrine of Avatāras appeared as in the [[Gita.|Gītā.]] The important works on bhakti are : The [[Narayapiya|Nārāyaṇīya]] section of the [[Santiparya|Śāntiparva]] (chapters 322–351 in the Citraśālā edition and chap. 322-339 of the critical edition ); the [[Bhagavadgitā;|Bhagavadgītā;]] several Purāṇas, the most important being Viṣṇu and [[Bhagavata ;|Bhāgavata;]]109 the Bhaktisūtra of [[Sandilya|Śāṇḍilya]] with the bhāṣya of Svapneśvara (ed. by Jivananda, Calcutta 1876) and translation of both these ( in B. I. series ) by E. B. Cowell [[P958]] (1878); [[Śandilya’s|Śāṇḍilya’s]] [[samhita|saṁhitā]] ( [[Bhaktikhanda)|Bhaktikhaṇḍa)]] in the [[Sarasvati bhavan|Sarasvatībhavan]] Series, edited by Anantasastri Phadke, (1935); [[Nārada bhaktisūtra|Nārada-bhaktisūtra]] with English Translation by Nandlal Sinha ([[Panini|Pāṇini]] office, Allahabad, 1911); [[tho|the]] Nārada-pāñcarātra ( containing the [[Jõānāmrtasāra|Jñānāmṛtasāra]] section) in [[sloven|eleven]] chapters ( ed. for B. I. Series by [[O.|C.]] M. Banerji, Calcutta, 1865 ) and English translation of it by Swami Vijñānānanda (Pāṇini Office, Allahabad, 1921); Sir R. G. [[Bhadarkar’s|Bhandarkar’s]] [[“Vaignavigm,|‘Vaiṣṇavism,]] [[Saivism|Śaivism]] &c.’ (1913, in the [[Enoyclopaedia|Encyclopaedia]] of Indo-Aryan Research ); ‘Das-Gupta’s History of Indian Philosophy’ vol. IV. (1949), wherein he deals with the Bhāgavatapurāṇa and the doctrines of [[Madhya,|Madhva,]] Vallabha, Caitanya and their followers; Grierson’s paper [[Gleanings|‘Gleanings]] from Bhaktamāla of Nābhādāsa’, in J. R. A. S. for 1909 pp. 607-644; [[• History|‘History]] of [[Śrivaiṣṇavas’,|Śrīvaiṣṇavas’,]] by T. A. Gopinatha Rao (Madras, [[1993),|1923),]] ’the Gospel of Nārada’ by Duncan Greenless (Adyar, 1951); [[Narada-bhakti-sūtras|Nārada-bhakti-sūtras]] ( text, translation and notes ) ed. by Swami [[Tyagisapanda|Tyāgīśānanda]] (Ramakrisna Math, Mylapore, Madras, 1943 ) in five adhyāyas and 84 sūtras; the [[Abirbudhnya-samhita|Ahirbudhnya-saṁhitā]] in two volumes ( Adyar, 1916 ); [[Di,|Dr.]] F. Otto Schrader’s Introduction to the [[Pañcarātra|Pāñcarātra]] and [[Abirbudhnya-gamhita|Ahirbudhnya-saṁhitā]] (Adyar, 1916 ); [[Jayakhya-samhita|Jayākhya-saṁhitā]] with English and Sanskrit Introductions [[(G.O. 9.|(G. O. S.]] 1931); the [[Parama-samhita|Parama-saṁhitā]] [[(G.0. 8.,|(G. O. S.,]] 1946 ) with English Translation by Dr. S. [[K,|K.]] Aiyangar; the [[Bșhadbrahmanamhita|Bṛhadbrahmasaṁhitā]] (of [[Narada-pāžoarātra|Nārada-pāñcarātra]] ) in the [[Anandasrama|Ānandāśrama]] series, 1912); [[Bhakticandrika|Bhakticandrikā]] ( commentary on [[Sandilya’s|Śāṇḍilya’s]] [[Bhaktisātras)|Bhaktisūtras)]] by [[Narayanatirtha|Nārāyaṇatīrtha]] ( [[Sarasvatibhavan|Sarasvatībhavan]] series, 1921, 1938 ); [[Bhakti prakāśa|Bhaktiprakāśa]] of Mitramiśra ( Chowkhamba Series, 1934 ); [[Bhakti nirṇaya|Bhaktinirṇaya]] of [[Anantadora|Anantadeva]] (ed. by Pandit Anantasastri Phadke, Banaras, 1937). There is a good deal of Bhakti literature in South India, such as the hymns of the [[Alwārs,|Āḷwārs,]] but no reference is made here to it for several reasons.

Before proceeding with the treatment of bhakti in the Purāṇas the words ‘bhakti’ and [[‘Bhagavata’|‘Bhāgavata’]] must be briefly defined and explained. [[Sāṇdilya|Śāṇḍilya]] defines110 ‘bhakti’ as [[’s|‘sā]] [[P959]] [[paranuraktirisyare’.|parānuraktirīśvare’.]] This may be interpreted in two [[ways the|ways ’the]] highest form of bhakti is affection fixed on God’ or ‘bhakti is the highest affection fixed on God’. [[Svapnegyara,|Svapneśvara,]] the author of a [[bhāsya|bhāṣya]] on [[Sandilya,|Śāṇḍilya,]] prefers the first and [[Narada-bhakti sūtra,|Nārada-bhakti-sūtra,]] Tilak and others favour the second. [[Svapnesvara|Svapneśvara]] explains that in general ‘bhakti’ means ‘affection fixed on a being that is to be won over or worshipped’, but that in this [[sastra|śāstra]] it means ‘a particular state of the mind having the Supreme Lord as its object’ and quotes [[& verge|a verse]] from the Viṣṇupurāṇa uttered by the great devotee [[Prahlada May|Prahlāda ‘May]] that fixed (or unwavering) love, which the ignorant (or unreflecting) people feel for worldly objects, [[nover|never]] depart from my heart, ever remembering you’. The [[Gitā|Gītā]] employs the word [[‘priti’|‘prīti’]] (affection) and conveys that the word ‘bhakti’ is derived from the root ‘bhaj’ to resort [[to, ‘Those|to. ‘Those]] whose minds are on me, whose lives are offered to me, who instruct each other, who speak of me, are always contented and happy. On these who are continuously devoted to me and serve me with affection, I bestow that knowledge by which they [[roach|reach]] me’. [[Svapnośvara|Svapneśvara]] explains that the word ‘anurakti’ (with prefix ‘anu’) is employed to convey that the affection for God arises after the devotee secures knowledge of the greatness and other attributes of the Bhagavat (Adorable One). In the Viṣṇupurāṇa the word ‘anurāga’ is used for ‘bhakti’, where, after describing the ascent of Rāma and his brothers to heaven, it says that the people of the capital of Kosala who had deep affection for those incarnate parts of the Bhagavat [[(Viṣṇu )|(Viṣṇu),]] having their minds fixed on them, reached the position of residence in the same world with them. [[Sārdilya|Śāṇḍilya]] further111 says that there is the teaching that there is immortality for him who abides in Him. In the [[Chandogya|Chāndogya]] Up. it is said ‘He who abides in brahman reaches immortality’. The idea is that immortality being the promise of abiding in God, there will be no indifference in the effort to know God or in the effort to [[P960]] cultivate highest affection for God. It may be noticed that the sūtras of Nārada appear to be a [[more|mere]] paraphrase of [[Sandilya’s|Śāṇḍilya’s]] [[aphoriams.|aphorisms.]]112 [[Sandilya|Śāṇḍilya]] [[furthor|further]] (sūtra 7) provides that bhakti, like knowledge, is not an action because it does not follow an effort of the will and that (sūtra 9) it is different from jñāna, as the [[Gitā|Gītā]] refers to self-surrender being attained by one having knowledge after many births [[(Gitā VII 19).|(Gītā VII. 19).]]

Our ancestors had a great penchant for classifications, divisions and sub-divisions. Bhakti is divided into [[laukiki|laukikī]] (of the common people), [[Vaidiki|Vaidikī]] (laid down by the Veda) and [[adhyatmiki|ādhyātmikī]] (philosophical), as in Padma V. 15. 164; or [[mānasi|mānasī]] (mental), [[vāciki|vācikī]] (verbal) and [[kāyiki|kāyikī]] ( done with the body, such [[es|as]] fasts, vratas &c.) in Padma V. 15. 165–168; into [[Sāttviki,|Sāttvikī,]] [[Rājasi|Rājasī]] and [[Tāmasi|Tāmasī]] (as in [[Bhagavata|Bhāgavata]] III. 29.7–10 and Padma VI. 126. 4-11); into best, middling and inferior as in [[Brahmānda|Brahmāṇḍa]] III.113 34. 38-41 ).

Prapatti (self-surrender) is distinguished from bhakti in works of the Rāmānuja and of other Vaiṣṇava schools. It consists of five points114 viz. resolution to yield (to God’s will), the abandoning of opposition, faith that God will protect (the devotee ), praying to God to save the devotee, and a feeling of helplessness shown by casting one’s soul on Him. Bhakti [[bas As|has as]] synonyms the [[words’ dhyāna’,|words ‘dhyāna’,]] ‘upāsanā’ [[&o.|&c.]] and is subsidiary to prapatti. The [[Gita|Gītā]] explicitly makes no such distinction. In [[Gita|Gītā]] II. 7 Arjuna speaks of himself as ‘prapanna’ (who has approached or surrendered himself for salvation). The final advice at the end of the [[Gitā|Gītā]] [[enjoing|enjoins]] what is called prapatti in later works ‘On me fix your mind, become my devotee, sacrifice [[P961]] to me, offer adoration [[tome;|to me;]] you will certainly reach me; I declare to you truly, you are dear to me. Giving up all (dharmas) duties, come to me as your (only) refuge; I shall release you from all [[ging;|sins;]] do not [[griove :|grieve’.]]115 Vide also [[Gita VII,|Gītā VII.]] 14, 15 and XV. 4 for other instances of the use of the root ‘pra-pad’. The theory propounded in the Gītā and other works on bhakti was that bhakti led on to [[‘prasada’|‘prasāda’]] (favour or grace) of God which enables the devotee to attain mokṣa.116 The [[Gitā|Gītā]] (18.56, 58, 62 ) says ‘a man, though always performing all actions, but solely depending on me, obtains an imperishable and eternal place through my favour; if you fix your mind on me, you will get over all difficulties through my favour; go to Him as the refuge with all your heart, O Arjuna, through His grace you will attain the highest peace and an everlasting [[abode.|abode.’]] In the [[Vispupurāṇa|Viṣṇupurāṇa]] Prahlāda117 is told by the Adorable [[One as|One ‘as]] your mind is firmly and devotedly fixed on me you will by my favour attain the highest bliss’. The idea of God’s grace [[oocurs|occurs]] both in the [[Katha|Kaṭha]] and [[Svetāśvatara|Śvetāśvatara]] Upaniṣads :118 [[The|‘The]] Self, smaller than the small and greater than the great, is hidden in the heart of the creatures; a man who is free from willing anything and free from grief sees the greatness of the Self through the favour of the Creator’.

There is a great difference between the [[Gita|Gītā]] and the [[Nārāyaniya|Nārāyaṇīya]] section. In the former, though the Supreme Soul is called [[Vasudeva,|Vāsudeva,]]119 the doctrine of the four [[vyūbas,|vyūhas,]] that is very [[P962]] characteristic of the [[Nārāyaniya|Nārāyaṇīya]] section, is totally [[absont|absent]] and further even the names of [[Saṅkarṣana,|Saṅkarṣaṇa,]] Pradyumna and [[Ani ruddha|Aniruddha]] do not occur in the [[Gita.|Gītā.]] In my opinion the [[Gita|Gītā]] is the older of the two, as it propounds the general doctrine of bhakti, while the Pāñcarātra doctrine in the [[Nārāyaniya|Nārāyaṇīya]] is only one of the several bhakti schools. Moreover, the [[Nārāyaplya|Nārāyaṇīya]] section represents that [[Gita|Gītā]] had already been proclaimed and that the knowledge brought from [[Śyetadvipa|Śvetadvīpa]] by Nārada is the same as that declared in the [[Harigita|Harigītā]] (chap. 346. 10–11, 348. 53-54). [[Santi|Śānti]] ( 348. 55-57 ) mentions that there was only one vyūha or there were two, three or four and that the Ekāntins attached great importance to [[ahimsā.|ahiṁsā.]] Besides, the worship of [[Vābudova|Vāsudeva]] is older than Pāṇini, since120 Pāṇini teaches the formation of the word [[Vasudevaka|Vāsudevaka]] as meaning ‘one whose object of worship is [[Vasudeva’.|Vāsudeva’.]] Vide Dr. Bhandarkar’s [[Vaiṣṇavism,|‘Vaiṣṇavism,]] [[Saivism|Śaivism]] &c.’ paragraphs 2-10 (pp. 3–19 of vol. IV of the collected works) for the antiquity of [[Vasudeva|Vāsudeva]] worship. The general view of most medieval writers on Dharmaśāstra about [[Pancarātra|Pāñcarātra]] is represented by the Pārijāta quoted in the K. R. that the [[Panca rātra|Pāñcarātra]]121 and Pāśupata śāstras are authoritative only so far as they are not opposed to the Vedas. This was the view of the [[Sūtasambitā|Sūtasaṁhitā]] also, on which a commentary was composed by the famous [[Madhavācārya.|Mādhavācārya.]]

In some Purāṇas the word [[Vasudeva|Vāsudeva]] is not derived122 from Vasudeva ( as the son of Vasudeva) but from the root [[yas’|‘vas’]] to [[dwell..|dwell.]] [[Vāsudeva|‘Vāsudeva]] is so called because all beings dwell in the Highest Self and [[Vasudeva|Vāsudeva]] dwells in beings as the soul of all’. Compare [[Gitā|Gītā]] 9. [[29’I|29 ‘I]] am alike to all beings; none is odious to me nor dear; but those who worship me with faith dwell in me and I too dwell in them’.

[[P963]] The word bhagavat [[oalls|calls]] for a brief notice. It was generally applied to [[Vasudeva.|Vāsudeva.]] The [[Viṣṇupuriṇa|Viṣṇupurāṇa]] [[says The|says ‘The]] word bhaga is applied to the six qualities collectively viz. fulness of sway, manliness (or energy), glory, auspiciousness, knowledge and indifference to [[wordly|worldly]] objects. This noble word bhagavān applies to [[Vasudeva|Vāsudeva]] who is the Highest Brahma and to no one else’.123 The [[Viṣṇupurana|Viṣṇupurāṇa]]124 further states that the word [[+ bhagavat’|‘bhagavat’]] may be applied secondarily to others on the ground of possessing special qualities " that person may be [[oalled|called]] ‘bhagavān’, who knows about the creation and dissolution (of the world), about the origin and final destiny of beings, and knows what is [[vidya|vidyā]] and avidyā ( nescience). Knowledge, strength power, sway, manliness, [[splendour all|splendour—all]] these in their entirety, excluding the [[thres|three]] guṇas (and their effects ) that are to be avoided, are expressed by the word bhagavat”. [[Bhagavata|Bhāgavata]] is one who worships [[‘Bhagavat’(ie. Vasudeva).|‘Bhagavat’ (i. e. Vāsudeva).]] This is an ancient word. It [[ocours|occurs]] in the [[Begnagar|Besnagar]] column inscription [[of|(of]] the 2nd century [[BC.)|B. C.)]] of Heliodora, a Greek of [[Takṣaśila|Takṣaśilā]] and ambassador of Antalikita, who calls himself a [[bhagavata|bhāgavata]] [[(adevotee|(a devotee]] of [[Vasudeva);|Vāsudeva);]] vide above p. 516 n. 742 and ‘Indo-Greeks’ by Prof. A. [[K,|K.]] Narain (1957), where at end [[hegives|he gives]] the full Besnagara Ins. of nine lines and its reading (Plate VI.). It appears that [[‘bhagayat’|‘bhagavat’]] was rarely applied to [[Siya|Śiva]] also. The [[Svetāśvatara|Śvetāśvatara]] Upaniṣad speaks125 of [[Sive|Śiva]] as [[‘bhagavan’.|‘bhagavān’.]] [[Patanjali|Patañjali]] in his [[bhasya|bhāṣya]] on [[Panini|Pāṇini]] [[V.2.76|V. 2. 76]] speaks of [[Śivabhagavata.|Śivabhāgavata.]]126 In the [[Ghostindi|Ghosūṇḍī]] stone [[P964]] Inscription127 in Sanskrit ( near [[Nagari|Nagarī]] in the Chitorgadh District of Rajputana ) there is a reference to both Saṅkarṣaṇa and [[Vasudeva|Vāsudeva]] as [[bhagarat|bhagavat]] and it calls them Sarveśvara (about 200 B. C.), while the Besnagara inscription speaks of [[Vāsudova|Vāsudeva]] alone and Heliodora calls himself [[bbāgavata.|bhāgavata.]] In some early records such as the Pikira grant of [[Simbavarman|Siṁhavarman]] ( E. I. vol. VII. p. 161 ) and in Gupta Inscription No. 4 (at p. 27) [[Simbavarinen|Siṁhavarman]] and Candragupta (II) son of Samudragupta are called [[paramabhāgauna’.|‘paramabhāgavata’.]] Akrūra is styled mahābhāgavata in [[Brahma purāṇa|Brahmapurāṇa]] [[190, 20;|190. 20;]] Padma VI. 280. 27 defines a [[Mahabhāgavata|Mahābhāgavata]] as noted below.128

Three mārgas ( paths) are spoken of in ancient works viz. karmamārga, bhaktimārga and jñānamārga. It is necessary to say here something about the path of [[bhakli|bhakti]] and that of [[jñana.|jñāna.]] Both these paths are deemed to lead to the same goal, [[viz|viz.]] mokṣa. The mode of approach in the two is, however, [[different,|different.]] In the path of knowledge (or [[ayaktajāsanā)|avyaktopāsanā)]] it is not bare book knowledge of brahman as the Supreme Soul and as [[nirguna|nirguṇa]] that will lead to mokṣa; for that purpose [[wbat|what]] is required is the [[brāhmi-sthiti|brāhmī-sthiti]] (state of identifying oneself with brahma) [[men. tioned|mentioned]] in [[Gitā|Gītā]] II. 72. This condition can be secured only by great efforts and long practice as described in [[Gitā|Gītā]] II. 55 and the following verses. In the path of knowledge whatever actions the person concerned may do are [[brahmārpana,|brahmārpaṇa,]] as described in [[Gita|Gītā]] IV. 18–24. In the path of bhakti the bhakta resigns [[him self|himself]] to God’s grace and whatever [[be|he]] does [[Le|he]] [[consigos|consigns]] to the God worshipped by him such as [[Vasudeva|Vāsudeva]] ([[saguna|saguṇa]] and [[vyakla).|vyakta).]] Arjuna asks the Lord a question in [[Gitā XII, 1|Gītā XII. 1]] [[of|‘of]] the [[worship pers|worshippers]] who thus constantly devoted meditate on you and those [[wbo|who]] (meditate ) on the [[Unparceived|Unperceived]] and Indestructible, which [[P965]] best know devotion’. The reply given in XII. 2-7 [[is those|is ’those]] who, being constantly devoted and [[possogged|possessed]] of the highest devotion, worship me with a mind fixed on me are regarded by me as the most devoted. But those who, restraining the group of the senses, and with a mind equable to all, meditate on the [[indes cribable,|indescribable,]] indestructible, [[udperceived|unperceived]] (Principle), which is [[all pervading,|all-pervading,]] inconceivable, unconcerned, immoveable, and constant, they, devoted to the good of all beings, certainly attain to me. In the case of those whose minds are attached to the unperceived ( Principle or Spirit) the trouble is much greater, since the unperceived goal is attained by embodied beings with difficulty. In the case of those, however, who dedicating all their actions to me and regarding me as their highest goal worship me, meditating on me with a devotion towards none beside me and whose minds are placed on me, I, without delay, become their deliverer from the ocean of saṁsāra and death’. In chapter 9 the path of bhakti is spoken of in these [[terms it|terms ‘it]] is the chief among vidyās (lores), chief among mysteries; it is the best means of sanctification; it can be directly apprehended, it is in accordance with dharma, imperishable and easy to practise’. According to the [[Gitā,|Gītā,]] therefore, the path of bhakti is easier than the path of knowledge.

The [[Bhagavata|Bhāgavata]] says that bhakti is ninefold129 [[viz,|viz.]] hearing about Viṣṇu, repeating his name, remembering him, worshipping the feet (of the image of Viṣṇu), offering [[pūja,|pūjā,]] bowing (or homage), treating oneself as the slave of Viṣṇu, treating Him as a friend and surrendering one’s soul to Him. According130 to [[Narada-bhakti-sūtra|Nārada-bhakti-sūtra]] it is elevenfold (as noted below). It is [[P966]] not to be supposed that all these nine methods have to be [[pra otised|practised]] at the same time. A devotee practising any one of these, [[vir,|viz.]] remembering or reciting His name, may thereby become [[&|a]] true bhakta, and may win God’s favour and secure liberation [[(Sandilyasūtra|(Śāṇḍilyasūtra]] 73). The [[Gitā|Gītā]] (VII. 16-17) states ‘four classes of men, who are (all) fortunate, worship me viz. one who is in distress, the seeker after knowledge, one who seeks some desired object (or seeks wealth), one who is possessed of knowledge; of these he who is possessed of knowledge, who is always devoted and who worships One (Being) only is distinguished (above others ), for to the man of knowledge I am extremely dear and he is dear to me’. [[Sāedilya|Śāṇḍilya]] provides that the four aspects (out of nine) viz. remembering God, reciting His name, [[Darrating|narrating]] stories about Him and bowing to Him (His [[imago),|image),]] find their place in the bhakti of those who are distressed, for these are said to be the means of expiation of sins, as the Viṣṇupurāṇa says (II. 6. 39) that remembering [[Krsna|Kṛṣṇa]] is superior to all [[praya ścittas.|prāyaścittas.]] [[Sandilya|Śāṇḍilya]] further says that those guilty of mortal (grave) sins are only entitled to the [[bbakti|bhakti]] of the distressed; but when their sins have been wiped off they would be entitled to the other forms of bhakti.

[[Gitā|Gītā]] does not expressly [[enumerato|enumerate]] all [[thega|these]] nine modes of bhakti, but most of them can be gathered from various passages of the [[Gitā|Gītā]] such as IX. 14, 26, 27 and from passages in other Purāṇas. For example, the Viṣṇupurāṇa131 says ‘whatever be the expiatory rites, consisting of austerities, deeds of charity [[&o.,|&c.,]] to [[romembor|remember]] [[Krṣna|Kṛṣṇa]] is superior to them all’ and ’that repetition of His name with bhakti is the best solvent of all sins, as fire is of metals’. In the [[Bhagavata “571|Bhāgavata]]132 it is said ‘whatever the devotee does by his body, speech, mind, organs of sense, by his [[intelleot|intellect]] or by his soul or by the force of temperament that he [[follow|follows—]] all that he should offer to Nārāyaṇa who is highest’. This is entirely in line with [[Gitā|Gītā]] IX. 27 and may be called [[*dāsya-bhakti’;|‘dāsya-bhakti’;]] while Arjuna’s bhakti is ‘sakhya-bhakti’, [[28|as]] Lord [[Krspa|Kṛṣṇa]] [[himselt|himself]] speaks of him as ‘my devotee and a friend’ [[(Gita|(Gītā]] [[P967]] [[IV.3).|IV. 3).]] It appears that the [[Gits|Gītā]] regards the performance133 of the duties of his station in life by a bhakta as worship (arcana or [[pūja)|pūjā)]] of God ‘worshipping by the performance of one’s duties (without an eye to the fruit or [[towards|rewards]] of them ) God from whom the world springs and by whom all this world is permeated, a man obtains perfection (and not only by offering flowers or by reciting the name of God).

This central doctrine of the [[Gitā|Gītā]] of what is called [[’niskām karma’|‘niṣkāma-karma’]] was accepted by the [[Purāṇas,|Purāṇas.]] The Viṣṇu [[II, 3.25|II. 3. 25]] adopts it. The Agnipurāṇa in chapter 381 gives a summary of [[the . Gitā|the Gītā]] in 58 verses which are mostly made up of passages from the [[Gitā.|Gītā.]] It winds up the summary with the verse quoted in note 1575 and emphasizes bhakti in the last verse.134 The Garuḍa-purāṇa summarises the Gītā in 28 verses (I. 237-238). The Padma (in VI. 171-188) contains the [[mahatmya|māhātmya]] of each of the 18 chapters of the [[Gitā|Gītā]] interspersed with legends about the fruit of reading each [[Gitā|Gītā]] chapter (1005 verses in all). A few further examples may be cited. The Kūrma [[saya|says]]135 ’therefore even [[&|a]] man not possessed of (high) wisdom should perform all his duties (or actions ) abandoning with all efforts the fruit (or [[roward)|reward)]] of actions; he obtains (high) abode after some time’; [[*actions|‘actions]] done after offering them to me do not tend to bind down the [[door|doer]] but tend to mukti [[(liberation).|(liberation)’.]] The [[Mārkandeya|Mārkaṇḍeya]] also refers to [[’niskāma-karma’.|‘niṣkāma-karma’.]] The [[Bhagavata-purāña|Bhāgavata-purāṇa]] provides ‘a man performing (the rites) [[proscribed|prescribed]] by the Veda but [[with out|without]] attachment (not caring for the reward) and surrendering them to God [[Secures|secures]] freedom from the bondage of karma and the [[deolaration|declaration]] of the [[roward|reward]] (in the Veda) is only meant for stimulating effort.’

The doctrine of [[adraila|advaita]] in the Upaniṣads (such as in [[Isa|Īśā]] 16, Tai. Up. III. 4 and 8, Bṛ. Up. II. 4. 14, IV. 3. 30-31, IV. 5. 15) was for the wise. They offer very little to the ordinary man [[P968]] about God or the ultimate destiny of man or the way to God and did not solve the common man’s problems. The [[Gita|Gītā]] took up the problems of the common man; it shows to the lowliest of the lowly that there is hope for him, that the One and the True Being can enter into his life, if he consecrates all the daily duties and [[Botions|actions]] of his station in life to God and that salvation will come to every one if one has loving faith in God and surrenders [[him. self|himself]] entirely to his Grace. The [[Gita|Gītā]] proclaims (IX. 30-32) ‘If even a man that has been very badly conducted worships me without worshipping any one else he should be regarded as a good man, since he has resolved upon the right course; he quickly becomes a righteous soul and reaches everlasting peace: O Arjuna! you may affirm that my devotee is never ruined. By taking refuge with me even those that are born in despised castes, also women, vaiśyas and [[sūdras|śūdras]] reach the highest goal’. The [[Sandilya-bhakti-sūtra|Śāṇḍilya-bhakti-sūtra]] provides136 that all down to persons of the despised [[castos|castes]] are entitled (to follow the path of bhakti) as they are capable of learning bhakti at second hand just [[ag|as]] they can learn the common rules (of [[ahimsā,|ahiṁsā,]] truthfulness [[&o.).|&c.).]] The [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] speak in the same strain as the [[Gita|Gītā]] and are [[some times|sometimes]] even more explicit and emphatic. The Brahma-purāṇa paraphrases [[Gita IX, 32|Gītā IX. 32]] and proceeds ‘my devotee even if he be [[& candāla|a cāṇḍāla]] attains the desired beatitude, if he be endowed with the right faith; what need be said about others?’ The Padma137 [[states A|states ‘A]] Pulkasa or even a [[svāpaka|śvāpaka]] and other persons [[belong. ing|belonging]] to Mleccha tribes become eminent and worthy of being honoured, if they are solely devoted to the worship of the feet of Hari’; [[’ Even|‘Even]] a śvapāka is a Vaiṣṇava if on his lips there is the name of Hari, in whose heart there is [[Visgu|Viṣṇu]] and in whose stomach goes food offered to Viṣṇu’. The [[Bhagavata|Bhāgavata]] [[containgist|contains]]138 [[P969]] the following remarkable [[statement: Homage|statement: ‘Homage]] to that Supreme Lord, by resorting to whom as refuge Kirātas139 [[(mountain dwellers|(mountain-dwellers]] like Bhils), the Hūṇas, Andhras, Pulindas, Pulkasas, [[Abhiras,|Ābhīras,]] [[Kankas,|Kaṅkas,]] Yavanas, Khasas and the like and other degraded men are purified.’ These sentiments were not mere platitudes, but had been very largely acted upon. Even in the medieval ages in India we have women saints like [[Mirabai|Mirābai]] and [[Āṇdāļ|Āṇḍāḷ]] (in South India), untouchables as saints like Nanda, [[Cokhāmeļā|Cokhāmeḷā]] (in [[Mahārāṣtra),|Mahārāṣṭra),]] Rai Das (a chāmār disciple of Rāmānanda), sinners like Ajāmila honoured as [[saints, Saints|saints. Saints]] like Kabir140 (a Moslem weaver) and [[Tukārām.had|Tukārām had]] probably not much book learning, but their hymns are recited by Hindus including orthodox brāhmaṇas.

The invasions and gradual conquest of India by Moslem [[in vaders|invaders]] from the Northwest [[throw|threw]] down a challenge to Hindus from 11th century A.D. onwards. It was met in various ways. The first was the composition of comprehensive digests of [[amstis|smṛtis]] of which the earliest extant one is the [[Kștyakalpataru|Kṛtyakalpataru]] of [[Lakṣmidhara|Lakṣmīdhara]] (about 1110-1130 A.D.) in the North and Hemādri in the [[Docoan|Deccan]] (third quarter of 13th century). The second and the most important way was on the spiritual front. From the 13th to the 17th century there was a great revival of spirituality on an unprecedented scale which produced saints and [[mystios|mystics]] in all parts of India such as [[Jõāneśvara,|Jñāneśvara,]] Nāmadeva, Rāmānanda, Kabir,140 Caitanya, [[P970]] [[Dadu|Dādu]] (in Rajasthan), [[Nanak,|Nānak,]] [[Vallabhacārya,|Vallabhācārya,]] Ekanātha, Tukārām, Rāmadāsa ( and many others of lesser [[famo)|fame)]] who all agreed on fundamentals viz. unity of God, the need of self-purification, [[com demnation|condemnation]] of the pride of caste and of formalities of worship, and [[Surrender|surrender]] to the Deity for salvation. The third was the creation of such independent kingdoms as that of Vijayanagara [[(1330|(1330–]] 1565 A. D.), of [[Mahārāstra|Mahārāṣṭra]] (under Shivaji and the Peshwas) and of the Sikhs in Punjab. This last cannot be dealt with way in this work. The doctrine of bhakti had a great appeal to all sections of the Hindu community and its propagation by the Purāṇas went a long [[away|way]] in weaning away Hindus from Buddhism. Not only so, Mahāyāna Buddhism took over the doctrine of bhakti and works like the [[Questions|‘Questions]] of Milinda’ and the [[Saddharmapuṇdarika|Saddharmapuṇḍarīka]]141 contain passages which agree closely with the [[Gita.|Gītā.]] The [[Gitā|Gītā]] exhibits a wonderful spirit of tolerance and accommodation, not found in the scriptures of other religions which were founded by great prophets. It says142 [[even|‘even]] those, who are devotees of other deities and worship them with faith, ( indirectly) worship me only but with a [[pon-śāstric|non-śāstric]] (or irregular ) procedure.’ The Bhāgavata-purāṇa elaborates the same [[idea; O|idea; ‘O]] Adorable One! others worship you alone in the form of Śiva while following the path promulgated by Śiva and propounded in different ways by several [[acūryas,|ācāryas,]] all worship you who are Lord and who comprehend in yourself all gods; those also who are devotees of other deities and appear to have different ideas do in the end reach you, just as all rivers rising in mountains and flooded by rains enter the ocean from all sides’. The [[Santiparva|Śāntiparva]]143 also contains a similar [[idea Those|idea ‘Those]] who worship [[Brahma,|Brahmā,]] Śiva or other deities and whose conduct (or practice ) is intelligent will (ultimately ) come to me, who am the Highest’. This doctrine has [[ita|its]] [[gorms|germs]] in the [[Roveda|Ṛgveda]]144 [[P971]] itself where it is [[said the|said ’the]] One Reality the [[Bages|sages]] speak of under [[Various|various]] names; they call it Agni, Yama, [[Matari van|Mātariśvan]] (wind-god )’. This work cannot afford, for reasons of space, to go into the several bhakti schools such as those of Rāmānuja, [[Madhya|Madhva]] (who postulated five eternal bhedas), Caitanya and Vallabha ( that gave rise to an erotico-mystical brand of bhakti ) and others.

In their [[zoal|zeal]] for the spread of the [[oult|cult]] of bhakti the Purāṇas are sometimes guilty of gross exaggeration. The [[Brahma purāṇa|Brahmapurāṇa]]145 says [[‘mon|‘men]] even after having committed many sins under the influence of error (or delusion ), do not go to Hell, if they worship Hari who removes all [[sing;|sins;]] those men also who always remember Janārdana, though they may be guilty of roguery, reach, after they die, the happy world of Viṣṇu. Even a man who is habituated to flying into extreme rage, if he recites the name of Hari, has his faults destroyed and attains mukti (liberation ) as the king of Cedi country did’. The [[Vāmana purāṇa|Vāmanapurāṇa]]146 observes “what has that person got to do with many mantras [[(i, e,|(i. e.]] he has no use for those), who is a bhakta of Viṣṇu ? The mantra ’namo Nārāyaṇāya’ is able to accomplish all objects. Success is for those who have bhakti for Viṣṇu; how can there be failure for them in whose heart is enthroned Janārdana dark like a blue lotus ?” The [[Vamana|Vāmana]] and Padma Purāṇas say that a man secures the same results by repeating the names of [[Viṣğu|Viṣṇu]] that he would secure by visiting all the [[tirthas|tīrthas]] ( sacred places) and holy [[shrinos|shrines]]147 in the world.

Many Purāṇas, particularly the Viṣṇu and the [[Bhāgavata, ·|Bhāgavata,]] are replete with the eulogies, the theory and practice of bhakti [[P972]] and illustrative stories about it. For reasons of space it is not possible to go into this matter at any length. A [[fow|few]] noteworthy points alone will be touched. A few [[versos|verses]] eulogising the [[Bhagavatapurāpa|Bhāgavatapurāṇa]] may be cited as samples of extreme [[exaggera tion.|exaggeration.]] [[Thousands|‘Thousands]]148 of Aśvamedhas and hundreds of [[Vājapoya|Vājapeya]] sacrifices are not equal to even the 16th part of the story narrated by [[Suka;|Śuka;]] he who always [[recitos|recites]] a half or a quarter verse of the [[Bhagavata|Bhāgavata]] [[secure|secures]] the merit of [[Rajasūya|Rājasūya]] and Aśvamedha; he who listens to the words of the śāstra of [[Suka|Śuka]] ( i.e. [[Bhagavata|Bhāgavata]] ) at the time of death, Govinda being pleased with him confers on him [[Vaikuntha;|Vaikuṇṭha;]] this is a good expiation for all sinners viz. uttering the name of Viṣṇu, since ( at that time) their mind has [[Viṣpu|Viṣṇu]] as the only object of thought’.149 Another point is the story of Ajāmila in the Bhāgavata (VI. 1. 20 ff and VI. 2), [[Padma(|Padma (]] [[L 31.|I. 31.]] 109 and VI. [[87.7|87. 7]] ) and other Purāṇas. Ajāmila150 ( who [[aban doned|abandoned]] his [[brāhmana|brāhmaṇa]] wife and kept a mistress) was a moral wreck addicted to gambling and thieving. When on his [[death bed|death-bed]] at the age of 80 he loudly called upon his youngest son named [[Nārāyana|Nārāyaṇa]] (out of ten sons born of the mistress ) with affection and recited that name and thought of that alone, he became free from his [[sing|sins]] and attained a high position difficult to attain’. Such stories have given rise to the common belief that the last thought at one’s death leads to a new birth [[appro priate|appropriate]] to that thought [[(unte|(ante]] matih sā [[gatih).|gatiḥ).]] The Upaniṣads contain the germ of the idea of the last thought; [[vide,|vide]] Chān. Up. [[III, 14, 1,|III. 14. 1,]] VIII. 2. 10, [[Bļ.|Bṛ.]] Up. IV. 4. 5. In the ‘Questions of Milinda’ (SBE vol. 35 pp. 123–124) this idea of the importance of the last thought is taken up. It may be that a single heart-felt invocation of the name of God, a single act of faith after [[P973]] repentance and complete surrender to God’s will, cancels the result of a whole life of crime and sin. This is the moral of Ajāmila’s [[atory,|story,]] but it is liable to be grossly misapprehended and might create a complacent belief that a man may commit as many sins in his life as possible, but if he remembers God at his death and repeats His name then, all sins of his are cleared away and wiped out. This is a rather dangerous doctrine. The [[Gita|Gītā]] ( VIII. 5-7) puts the matter in a clear light. [[“That|‘That]] person, who remembers me at the time of death and passes from the world leaving the body, attains my essence, there is no doubt about that. Whatever form a man remembers when he leaves the body, to that he goes, because he was always engrossed in that form. Therefore [[remem ber|remember]] me at all times and engage in battle; there is no doubt that having fixed your mind and intellect on me you will attain to me’. This passage of the [[Gṛtā|Gītā]] suggests that a person will remember the name of God at the time of his death only if he had been [[80|so]] doing all his life, while engaged in actively performing all his duties and not caring for the rewards thereof and that it is extremely rare or almost impossible for a man to remember God at the last moment when throughout his life he had been ungodly and a great sinner. The above idea is again emphasized in verses 10-13 of chap. VIII (of the [[Gitā)|Gītā)]] and in chap. [[XIII, 3|XVII. 3]] (yo yac-chraddhaḥ sa [[ova|eva]] saḥ).

In spite of the doctrine that God is one151 and the doctrine that whatever form of the deity one may worship, the worship reaches the Supreme Being, Vaiṣṇavas and Śaivas have been wrangling and abusing one another. For example, the [[P974]] [[Varāha purana|Varāhapurāṇa]] [[makos|makes]] Rudra declare the [[supremaoy|supremacy]] of Viṣṇu ([[obap.|chap.]]152 [[70.14|70. 14]] [[Narayanah|Nārāyaṇaḥ]] paro [[devah),|devaḥ),]] denounce [[Śaiva-siddhantas|Śaiva-siddhāntas]] that are outside the pale of the Vedas and put forward the outstanding view ’that such non-Vedic views were promulgated by Śiva himself at the request of Viṣṇu in order to delude [[people.|people’.]] Some Purāṇas started by saying that the Bauddhas and Jainas153 were asuras and enemies of gods that were purposely deluded by god. For example, the Matsya (24. 43-49) says that the sons of Raji deprived Indra of his kingdom and share in sacrifices, that [[Brhaspati,|Bṛhaspati,]] at Indra’s request, deluded the sons of Raji by composing a treatise of Jina-dharma which was opposed to the Veda and then Indra killed them. Vāyu 96. 230-32, Matsya 47. 11-12, [[Bhagavata|Bhāgavata]] I. 3. 24 appear to suggest that Viṣṇu himself deluded the people. The Agni (16. 1-4) also says with reference to the Bauddhas that Viṣṇu deluded them. The [[Viṣṇu purana|Viṣṇupurāṇa]]154 (III. 17-18) narrates that when the gods were [[P975]] defeated by the asuras (who also practised tapas and studied the Veda) they approached Viṣṇu and prayed to him to help them and to destroy the asuras and that then Viṣṇu [[produoed|produced]] from his body Māyā-moha (lit. who causes delusion by wiles) and made him over to the gods. Māyāmoha, who was naked, had shaved his head and held a bunch of peacock feathers in his hand, went to the asuras that were practising tapas on the banks of the Narmadā (III. 18. 1-2) and told them that if they followed his words they would secure mukti, led them away from the path of the [[Voda,|Veda,]] taught them formulas of sceptical reasoning and weaned them away from their dharma. Then he approached other asuras and taught them that [[sacri ficing|sacrificing]] animals was sinful, taught them nirvāṇa and the doctrines of [[vijnanavāda.|vijñānavāda.]] Some of the passages are very striking ‘In a short time the asuras were deluded by Māyāmoha and abandoned all concern with the path depending on the three Vedas. Some condemned the Vedas, some condemned the gods, while others condemned the body of sacrificial rites and brāhmaṇas. (They thought or said) The statement that killing an animal (in sacrifices) is desirable for the sake of Dharma (for accumulating merit) does not stand to reason; to say that offerings burnt by fire would conduce to reward (in the next world) is childish talk; ( if it be said that ) Indra attained the position of God by means of many sacrifices and enjoys the fuel-sticks of the [[sami|śamī]] tree, then an animal ( that subsists on leaves) is superior to Indra (who enjoys hard and thorny [[sami|śamī]] samidhs). If it is desired (by the Veda) that the animal killed in [[&|a]] sacrifice attains heaven, then why does not the sacrificer kill his own father in [[&|a]] sacrifice (and send him to heaven)? If one (the son) performs śrāddha (with the thought) that what is eaten by one (brāhmaṇa diner at śrāddha) tends to give satisfaction to another [[(i, e.|(i. e.]] the deceased father of the [[offeror|offerer]] of śrāddha), then travellers would not carry food ( on their [[baoka)|backs)]] which causes weariness to them.’ These are the arguments used by atheists [[(oārvākas).|(cārvākas).]] It is remarkable that even tāntrik works like the Kulārṇavatantra make Śiva say that he declared certain [[sāstras|śāstras]] purely for deluding certain bad people who did not know the Kaula dharma.155

From condemning the Jainas and Bauddhas some [[Puranas,|Purāṇas,]] entirely unmindful of what the Gītā declared ( in note 1584 ), [[P976]] proceeded so far as to say156 that a [[brāhmapa|brāhmaṇa]] who is not a [[Valspava|Vaiṣṇava]] is a heretic, that [[Vispu|Viṣṇu]] himself assuming the form of Buddha proclaimed a false [[sastra,|śāstra,]] and that all [[sastras|śāstras]] such as the [[Pasupata,|Pāśupata,]] [[Vaibeṣika|Vaiśeṣika]] of [[Kanāda,|Kaṇāda,]] Nyāya of Gautama, [[Saṅkhya|Sāṅkhya]] of Kapila, [[Carvaka|Cārvāka]] of [[Bṛbaspati|Bṛhaspati]] are [[tāmgan;|tāmasa;]] that the māyāvāda (of [[Śhaṅkara)|Śaṅkara)]] is a false [[sastra|śāstra]] and is disguised Bauddha (doctrine) and the extensive śāstra [[(Pūrvamlmā gā)|(Pūrvamīmāṁsā)]] of Jaimini is [[con demned,|condemned,]] since it made gods as of no use ( in his system ). The words of the Padma-purāṇa are ; ‘Listen O Goddess, while I declare to you in order the [[tā masa|tāmasa]] śāstras, by merely remembering which even wise men become [[singers.|sinners.]] First of all I promulgated [[Saiya|Śaiva]] [[sastras|śāstras]] such as the [[Pasupata|Pāśupata]] system, then the following were declared by brāhmaṇas that were possessed (or engrossed ) by my [[sakti|śakti]] (power); viz. Kaṇāda proclaimed the great Vaiśeṣika system, the Nyāya and [[Saṅkhya|Sāṅkhya]] systems were declared respectively by Gautama and Kapila : the much despised [[Carpāka|Cārvāka]] doctrine was declared by [[Bphaspati,|Bṛhaspati,]] while Viṣṇu, assuming the form of Buddha, promulgated, for bringing about the destruction of [[dailyas,|daityas,]] the false doctrine of [[Bauddbas|Bauddhas]] that go about naked or dressed in blue garments; I myself, O goddess, assuming the form of a [[brahmapa|brāhmaṇa]] declared in the Kali age the false [[gāstras|śāstras]] of the doctrine of Māyā which is bauddha in disguise. The brāhmaṇa Jaimini composed the great system of [[Pūrvamimāṁsa,|Pūrvamīmāṁsa,]] which is unmeaning on account of its atheistic discourse’. Vijñāna-bhikṣu in his [[Saṅkhya-pravacanabhāsya|Sāṅkhya-pravacanabhāṣya]] ( who flourished about 1550 A. D.) quotes eleven [[verges|verses]] from the Padma VI. 263 and holds the peculiar view that no śāstra that is [[ūstika|āstika]] (admits [[&|a]] soul ) is unauthoritative nor is there any contradiction, each śāstra being of full force and true in its own sphere. The original [[Saṅkhya|Sāṅkhya]] sūtra, on which he comments, tries to establish the impossible thesis that the teachings of the [[Saṅkhya|Sāṅkhya]] are not in real or irreconcilable contradiction with the doctrine of all-pervading oneness of brahma or with the doctrine that brahma is bliss (ānanda) or the system of theism (i. e. a personal God ). Sectarian exclusiveness and bigotry went [[80|so]] far that the [[Brabmanda|Brahmāṇḍa]]157 contains a dialogue between sage Agastya and [[P977]] [[Rama|Rāma]] in which it is said that the 108 names of [[Krsna|Kṛṣṇa]] (who is acknowledged by all to be an incarnation of Viṣṇu ) are so potent that the merit [[securgd|secured]] by repeating thrice the 1008 names of Viṣṇu is secured by repeating only once one of the 108 names (of [[Krsna|Kṛṣṇa]] ).158

The Viṣṇu and Padma do not stand alone in this [[strango|strange]] story of Viṣṇu himself or through Rudra teaching false doctrines for deluding the ungodly or those that dissented from or reviled the Vedas. There were other Purāṇas that sing the same tune; for example, the [[Kūrma-purāpa|Kūrma-purāṇa]] indulges in a vendetta against many śāstras and systems in several places. A few passages are set out here. [[(Devi|(Devī]] says) [[*|’]] the various159 [[sastras|śāstras]] that are found in this world and are opposed to the Vedas and [[smrtis|smṛtis]] are based on tamas (ignorance), viz. [[Kapāla,|Kāpāla,]] Bhairava, [[Yamala|Yāmala]] (a class of Tantra works), Vāma (left-hand practices of a class of Tantras), [[Arbata|Ārhata]] (Jain doctrines ), these and others are meant for deluding (persons ); I produced these [[sāstras|śāstras]] in another birth for deluding people’; ’therefore in order to protect (people) against those who were beyond the pale of the Vedas and for the destruction of sinners we shall, O Śiva, compose [[sastras|śāstras]] for deluding [[then%B|them;]] Rudra being thus advised by [[Madhava|Mādhava]] (Viṣṇu) composed śāstras that delude and Viṣṇu also urged on by Rudra did the same; [[P978]] (they produced the [[gāstras)|śāstras)]] called Kāpāla, [[Nakula,|Nākula,]]160 [[Vama.|Vāma,]] Bhairava (early and later), Pāñcarātra, Pāśupata and thousands of others; [[Śhaṅkara|Śaṅkara]] came down to the earth, wearing garlands of skull-bones and covered with ashes from a [[cometery|cemetery]] and wearing profuse matted [[pair,|hair,]] trying to delude this world and engaged in begging for the benefit of those’; [[One|‘One]] should161 not honour even by words [[Pancarūsra|Pāñcarātra]] and [[Pūśu pula|Pāśupata]] people that are heretics, following prohibited avocations and those who follow left-hand [[sākta|śākta]] practices; [[whon|when]] Buddhist mendicants, Nirgranthas, those who study the [[Pancarātra|Pāñcarātra]] doctrines, Kāpālikas, Pāśupatas and other similar heretics, being bad and deluded, eat the [[sraddha|śrāddha]] food (meant for a deceased person) that śrāddha would be of no use in this world and the next.’ For details of some of the [[horetical|heretical]] sects, vide [[. Heretical|‘Heretical]] sects in the [[Purāpas’|Purāṇas’]] by [[Sri|Śrī]] Radhakrishna Choudhary in [[A BORI|ABORI]] vol 37 (1956) pp. [[231-257.|234-257.]]

The Gītā (in chapter 16 ) speaks of two classes of people, viz. those born to godlike endowments and those born with demoniac qualities and describes the latter in verses 7-20. Some of the verses probably indicate that the reference is to atheists and the like; for verse 8 states [[they|‘they]] say that the world is devoid of truth [[P979]] [[(1. o.|(i. e.]] contains nothing that one can believe ), devoid of any fixed principle (such as virtue and vice ), devoid of a Ruler, and is produced by union brought about by lust and nothing else’. After describing their thoughts and aspirations the [[Gita|Gītā]] winds up [[these|‘these]] perform sacrifices which are so in name only with hypocrisy and against the prescribed procedure; they hate me in their own bodies and in those of others; these impure and cruel enemies I continually throw down in demoniac wombs; coming into demoniac wombs and being deluded, in each birth they go to vilest states without ever coming to me’. This is entirely different from what the Padma and other Purāṇas say about Pāśupatas, Pāñcarātras and about non-Vaiṣṇavas.

Both the [[Bhagavata|Bhāgavata]] and the Padma say that162 (the cult of) bhakti first arose in [[Dravida|Draviḍa]] country, it progressed or [[prosperad|prospered]] in the [[Karnātaka,|Karṇāṭaka,]] it was found in only a few places in [[Maha rastra|Mahārāṣṭra]] and declined in the Gurjara country; it was, on account of the terrible Kaliyuga, broken up by heresies and remained weak for a long time; but having reached Vṛndāvana ( near Mathurā ) it got a fresh start and assumed fine form. In book XI. the Bhāgavata again reverts to this assertion that in Kali people are solely devoted to Nārāyaṇa only in a few places but to the greatest extent in the [[Dravida|Draviḍa]] country where flow the rivers [[Tāmraparpi,|Tāmraparṇī,]] [[Kștamālā,|Kṛtamālā,]] [[Kaveri|Kāverī]] and [[Mahānadi|Mahānadī]] flowing to the west and states that those who drink the waters of these rivers are generally devotees of [[Vasudeva.|Vāsudeva.]]

Most great moral and spiritual upheavals have a tendency to reach [[&|a]] very low level in course of time. That appears to have happened in the case of bhāgavatas. The [[Atri-smrti|Atri-smṛti]] has [[&|a]] verse containing a [[sarcastio|sarcastic]] reference to bhāgavatas163 : [[Those|‘Those]] who are devoid of Vedic studies learn [[sāstras|śāstras]] ( such as grammar, [[Vedānta,|vedānta,]] logic [[&o.),|&c.),]] those who are wanting in [[śāstrio|śāstric]] lore become readers of Purāṇas for others; those who cannot be readers of Purāṇas become tillers; but those who are broken down even [[P980]] [[thore,|there,]] become bhāgavatas’. Atri appears to say that bhāgavatas are idlers, who do not study Veda or śāstra or who cannot even read [[Puranas|Purāṇas]] to [[othera|others]] for their livelihood or do not engage in agriculture and who pretend to be worshippers (or bhaktas[[ )|)]] of Viṣṇu or [[Krspa|Kṛṣṇa]] and fatten on what is given by others that are deluded into thinking that they have forsaken everything for the sake of their love of God. They become what are called [[’ buwās’|‘buwās’]] in [[Marāthi|Marāṭhi]] and other modern Indian languages.

Another striking development of the bhakti cult is the Erotic Mysticism (madhurā bhakti) associated with the worship of [[Kṭspa|Kṛṣṇa]] and of [[Rādha|Rādhā]] in that form of Vaiṣṇavism established by Caitanya and Vallabhācārya. For the Vaiṣṇava movement inspired by Caitanya, vide Dr. S. K. [[Do’s|De’s]] work on [[the|‘the]] Vaiṣṇava faith and movement in Bengal’ (Calcutta 1942) and the author’s ‘History of Sanskrit Poetics’ (1951) about [[Ujjvala nilamapi|Ujjvalanīlamaṇi]] of [[Rūpa-gosyamin|Rūpa-gosvāmin]] pp. 298-302. In the bhakti cult established by Vallabhācārya great importance is attached to the guru, who is one of the descendants of Vallabhācārya and to whom almost divine honours are paid. A wonderful [[develop mont|development]] of bhakti towards Rāma, regarded in the [[Rāmāyana|Rāmāyaṇa]] and in popular tradition as a paragon of restraint and all manly virtues, culminated in an erotic mysticism about [[Rama|Rāma]] and [[Sitā|Sītā]] also. The devotees of this mystic cult have to consider themselves as brides of Rāma or the female friends of [[Sitā,|Sītā,]] they are supposed to seek Lord Rāma’s favour through [[Sita,|Sītā,]] who graciously intercedes with the Lord for the devotees. Among the followers of Vallabhācārya the guru tells the devotee to look upon [[him the|him (the]] guru) as [[Krsna|Kṛṣṇa]] and upon himself or herself [[ag|as]] Rādhikā.

For want of space further details about these bhakti [[culte|cults]] have to be passed over.

Supreme importance is attached to Vedic mantras and also Paurāṇika mantras. They will have to be dealt with at some length in the sections on Tantras and on [[Pūrvamimāngā.|Pūrvamīmāṁsā.]] But some treatment of mantras, particularly vedic, may be given here as well. The word mantra occurs about twenty-five times in the [[Rgveda.|Ṛgveda.]] Only once does the word [[mantrakt|mantrakṛt]] occur in the [[Rgveda 1606|Ṛgveda]]164 (IX. [[114, 2)|114. 2)]] ‘O sage [[Kaśyapal|Kaśyapa!]] offer obeisance [[P981]] to king Soma Lord of plants with the chants of the composers (or authors) of mantras, thereby sending forth your own voice [[&o.’|&c.’]] Mr. Kapali Sastry is inaccurate when he states (on p. 67 of the translation of his [[‘Rgbhāsya-bhūmikā|‘Ṛgbhāṣya-bhūmikā]] in English) ‘[[wo|we]] see frequent mention made in the Rik [[Samhita|Saṁhitā]] of the rishi as the author of the mantra’ and he cites only [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] IX. 114. [[%|2]] and I. 67. 2. The latter contains no word like [[mantrakst.|mantrakṛt.]] [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 67. 2 (addressed to Agni) [[ning|runs]] ‘holding in his hand all powers (or riches), sitting in a cave (i. e. concealed) he placed the gods in fright; heroes (men or [[gods )|gods)]] that place [[(offor)|(offer)]] prayers know him (Agni) to be here when they recite mantras formed in the heart.’ There is no direct reference to [[psis|ṛṣis]] here (but to [[narah)|naraḥ)]] and what is emphasized is that mantras already extant as the heart’s outpourings enable the reciters of the mantras to find him (Agni). There is nothing to show that the mantras referred to in the verse are meant to be impromptu; the idea is rather that mantras that already existed and conveyed heartfelt devotion have to be employed to find Agni. The very next verse makes the position clear when it says, ’like the unborn (Sun) [[he ( Agni)|he (Agni)]] supports the wide earth and fixes in its place the sky on account of the mantras that turn out true’. This clarifies two things, viz. that when this mantra (I. 67. 3) was recited there already existed a host of [[mantras 1607|mantras]]165 and secondly, that the mantras that existed long before were deemed to have helped in supporting the earth and the sky. The ancient mantras were supposed to bring Indra to attend thrice in the day for a short time to the sacrifices of devotees when invoked with mantras addressed to him ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] III. 53. 8); similarly, mantras when recited by wise [[man|men]] bring Yama to receive offerings ([[ Rg. X. 14, 4);|Ṛg. X. 14. 4);]] [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 88. 14 states [[we|‘we]] raise our voices with mantras towards Vaiśvānara Agni, who is wise and who shines brilliantly all days’. The word [[mantra 1608|mantra]]166 is [[P982]] also employed in the singular several times. A few striking passages are set out here. ‘Indeed, God [[Brahmanaspati|Brahmaṇaspati]] (Lord of Prayer) proclaims the mantra fit for recitation (by the mouth of the [[hotr ),|hotṛ),]] in which the gods Indra, Varuṇa, Mitra, Aryaman make their abode, O [[Gods !’;|Gods!’;]] ‘We utter that very mantra [[( in|(in]] which Indra and other gods are praised together) in our sacrifices, the mantra being one that produces bliss and being free from all blemishes (or is [[incomparable );|incomparable);]] [[Do|‘Do]] place among the Gods a mantra that is not short, that is well-arranged and that is well ornamented; ancient fetters do not reach him who is in [[( the|(the]] good grace of) Indra by the sacrifice [[( or praise );|(or praise);]] ‘I address the same mantra for you (all) and offer for you [[( all)|(all)]] one offering (into fire)’. From these passages it appears that when these verses were recited there was a body of already existing mantras; which were supposed to be inspired by the Lord of prayers (or speech) and from which the choice of a long and well-formed one was to be made. Apart from the above passages the word mantra occurs in [[Rg. 1.|Ṛg. I.]] 31. 13, [[1. 74 1. 1. 147, 4,|I. 74. 1, I. 147. 4,]] I. 152. 2, II. 35. 2, [[VI,|VI.]] 50. 14, VII. [[7.6,|7. 6,]] X. 50. 4 and 6, X. 106. 11, in only one of which ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] II. [[35.2),|35. 2),]] apart from [[Rg. I,|Ṛg. I.]] [[67.2|67. 2]] already quoted, the words [[‘hṣda ā sutaztam mantram’(mantra|‘hṛdā ā sutaṣṭaṁ mantram’ (mantra]] well chiselled from the heart) [[ara|are]] used and in another verse more simply the word ‘atakṣan’ (in VII. [[7.6|7. 6]] mantram ye [[vāram|vāraṁ]] naryā atakṣan) alone is used. In two passages ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. [[95.1|95. 1]] and X. [[191, 3|191. 3]] first half) the word ‘mantra’ appears to mean ‘consultations, holding counsel together’. In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. [[20.4,|20. 4,]] [[Rbhus|Ṛbhus]] are called ‘satyamantrāḥ’ and are said to have made their parents young. There are controversies about what the [[Rbbus|Ṛbhus]] stand for and it is difficult to say what is meant by ‘satyamantrāḥ’ in relation to [[Rbhus.|Ṛbhus.]] [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] VII. 76. 4. [[is a 1609|is a]]167 somewhat enigmatic verse. It [[means they (Aogirases)|means ’they (Aṅgirases)]] alone, (our) ancient pitṛs, learned (or wise) and following the right path, enjoyed companionship among gods and they found out the Light (the Sun) concealed (by Svarbhānu, eclipse); they, whose mantras were true, brought forth Uṣas.’ In some passages where other words like [[sloma|stoma]] or brahma are used, there is [[P983]] mention of the stoma or brahma being made and polished by the devotee [[(0.g.|(e. g.]] X. 39. 14 ’etam vām [[stomam-Aéyināvakar mātakṣāma bhrgavo|stomam-Aśvināvakar-mātakṣāma bhṛgavo]] na ratham’). Vide [[Rg. I. 62.13|Ṛg. I. 62. 13]] [[for navyam|for ’navyam]] [[ataksad|atakṣad]] brahma’, [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] V. 29.15 (Indra brahma kriyamāṇā [[juṣa81% saviṣtha|juṣasva śaviṣṭha]] yā te navyā akarma); [[brahmakstaḥ|brahmakṛtaḥ]] (composers of prayers or praises) are mentioned in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] VII. [[32.2|32. 2]] and X. [[50.7.|50. 7.]] Other words like gir (several hundred times), [[dhiti|dhīti]] (about 100 [[times);|times),]] brahma (over a hundred times) mati (about 100 times), [[maniṣā|manīṣā]] (over 60 times), vacas and vacasyā (over 100 times), stoma (about 200 times), [[sukirti|sukīrti]] (five times), sūkta (four times) are employed in the sense of [[thought,|‘thought,]] word or thought out hymn or verse of praise’ and in several cases the sages of the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] state that it is a new verse or hymn of praise that they employ. Vide [[‘Davyasibhir-girbhiḥ’|‘navyasībhir-gīrbhiḥ’]] and ‘giram bhare [[navyasim jāya mānām’|navyasīm jāyamānām’]] in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] V. 42. 13., in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] VI. 49. 1, VII. [[53.2;|53. 2;]] [[Pra|‘Pra]] tavyasīm [[navyasim dhitimagnaye|navyasīm dhītimagnaye]]’ in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 143. 1; [[‘vaiśvānaraya|‘vaiśvānarāya]] [[matir-navyasi|matir-navyasī]] [[suciḥ|śuciḥ]] soma iva [[pavato’|pavate’]] ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] VI. 8. 1) and ‘iyam [[to|te]] [[navyast|navyasī]] matir agne [[adhāyyasmada’|adhāyyasmadā’]] ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] VIII. 74. 7); iyam te agne [[navyasi manīgā’|navyasī manīṣā’]] [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. [[4.6;|4. 6;]] [[“tā valgū 1610|‘tā valgū]]168 Dasrā [[purusa katamā|puruśākatamā]] pratnā navyasā [[vacasā vivāso|vacasā vivāse]]’ [[( Rg.|(Ṛg.]] VI. [[62,5);|62. 5);]] ‘[[Davyasi|navyasī]] [[sukirtiḥ’|sukīrtiḥ’]] [[ip|in]] [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. [[60.3;|60. 3;]] ‘sa pratnavat navyase [[visvavāra|viśvavāra]] sūktāya [[pathah krnuhi’|pathaḥ kṛṇuhi’]] in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] IX. 91. 5; ’nū [[navyase naviyase|navyase navīyase]] sūktāya sādhayā [[pathah’|pathaḥ’]] in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] [[LX.|IX.]] 9. 8. It is a remarkable fact that when words like [[‘sukirtiḥ’|‘sukīrtiḥ’]] and ‘sūkta’ that occur only four or five times in the whole of the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] are qualified by the word ’new or fresh’, the word ‘mantra’ occurring so many times does not even once bear the adjective ’new’. This emphasizes the conclusion stated above that in the times of many of the extant [[Rgvedic versos|Ṛgvedic verses]] mantras were a large group already existing, from which prayers were drawn as occasion required, though now and then new verses were added to those already existing. It may here be stated that in some places the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] looks upon prayers [[( |(]][[dhiti|dhīti]]) as divine and as on the same level with [[Asyins,|Aśvins,]] Uṣas, and the Sun [[( VIII. 35. 2 )|(VIII. 35. 2)]] and that ancient prayers [[wera|were]] inherited from ancestors [[(III. 39.2 seyamasme|(III. 39. 2 ‘seyamasme]] sanajā pitryā [[dhih’).|dhīḥ’).]]

Several [[hymng|hymns]] and verses of the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] are purely [[philo sophical,|philosophical,]] cosmological, mystic and speculative as I. [[164,|164.]] 4, 6, 29, [[P984]] 32, 39, 42; [[X, 71,|X. 71,]] X. 90 (Puruṣasūkta), X. 121 ([[Hiranyagarbha),|Hiraṇyagarbha),]] X. 129, [[X, 81-82|X. 81-82]] (Viśvakarman), X. 72, X. 125 (Vāk), X. 154 (state after death), X. 190 (creation).

About the meaning and purpose of Vedic mantras there are great controversies. This much may be said here that according to the [[Pūryamimāṁsā|Pūrvamīmāṁsā]] system the whole Veda is concerned with sacrifices, that Veda is divided into two classes, Mantra and [[Brāhmana,|Brāhmaṇa,]] that Vidhis (hortatory passages) are the most important part of the Veda, that a very large number of Vedic passages are [[mero|mere]] arthavādas [[( that|(that]] contain either praises of vidhis or are to be explained metaphorically or merely repeat what already exists or are of a legendary [[character )|character)]] and that mantras only serve the purpose of bringing to the mind of the [[sacrificor|sacrificer]] or priests what is to be done in a sacrifice and that the words used in the mantras ordinarily bear the [[game|same]] meaning as that in popular Sanskrit.

Long before [[Yaska ( several venturies|Yāska (several centuries]] before Christ) there were several schools of Vedic interpretation such as the [[Aitibāsikas ( who|Aitihāsikas (who]] acc. to Nir. II. 16 said that [[Vitra|Vṛtra]] was an Asura son of [[Trastr,|Tvaṣṭṛ,]] while acc. to the Nairuktas [[Vrtra moans|Vṛtra means]] only [[* cloud’|‘cloud’]] and the Veda contains metaphorical descriptions of battles, that the twins that [[Saranyu|Saraṇyu]] is said in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 17. [[%|2]] to have given up are according to the Nairuktas Indra and the Mādhyamikā-vāk, while according to the Aitihāsikas they are Yama and [[Yami,|Yamī,]] as stated in [[Nit, XII, 10),|Nir. XII. 10),]] the school of Naidānas is mentioned about ‘syāla’ and ‘sāma’ in Nir. [[VI|VI.]] 19, the school of ancient [[yājõikas|yājñikas]] in Nir. V. 11. [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 164. 32 [[(ya im cakāra )|(ya īṁ cakāra)]] is differently explained by the Parivrājakas [[( ascetic|(ascetic]] school) and the Nairuktas [[( etymologists).|(etymologists).]] The Nirukta also mentions the names of seventeen individual predecessors [[( from|(from]] whom it differs often and who differ among [[themselves )|themselves)]] such as Āgrāyaṇa, [[Audumbarayana,|Audumbarāyaṇa,]] Kautsa, Gārgya, Gālava, [[Sākatā yana,|Śākaṭāyana,]] [[Sākapūṇi.|Śākapūṇi.]] There are several mantras of which two different meanings are given in the Nirukta as in Nir. V. 11 on [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] VIII. [[77.4.|77. 4.]] In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 164 there are several verses with two meanings or more e. g. verse 21 (yatrā [[suparnā|suparṇā]] &c.) has two meanings [[(aco.|(acc.]] to Nir. III. [[13 )|13)]] one relating to the devatā Sūrya ([[adhidairata|adhidaivata]][[ )|)]] and the other spiritual [[( adhyātma );|(adhyātma);]] similarly, verse 32 of the same hymn has two meanings in the Nirukta (II. 8); the verse 39 [[(rco|(ṛco]] akṣare parame vyoman) is explained in four ways by Sāyaṇa; verse 41 [[( gaurir-mimāya)|(gaurīr-mimāya)]] is explained by Sāyaṇa in two ways, both being different from [[Yagka’s|Yāska’s]] explanation [[( in|(in]] Nir. [[P985]] [[XI, 40 );|XI. 40);]] verse 45 [[( oatvāri|(catvāri]] vāk parimitā [[padani)|padāni)]] is explained in six ways (more or less different from each [[other )|other)]] by Sāyaṇa; it is also explained in the Mahābhāṣya (p. 7 of [[M.M. Abhyankara. gastri’s|M. M. Abhyankara-śāstri’s]] ed.). The [[verse’ catvāri|verse ‘catvāri]] [[śrógā’|śṛṅgā’]] ([[Rg.|Ṛg.]] IV. 58. 3) has been the subject of varying explanations from very early times. [[Nir, (XIII, 7)|Nir. (XIII. 7)]] explains it as referring to Yajña. The [[Mahābhāgya|Mahābhāṣya]] (p. 6 of the Mahābhāṣya ibid.) also does the same. [[Sayapa|Sāyaṇa]] explains it as referring to Agni [[( identified|(identified]] with yajña) and Sūrya. This verse is a riddle. [[Sabara 1611|Śabara]]169 in his [[bhāsya|bhāṣya]] on [[P.M.S.|P. M. S.]] I. 2. 38. (‘abhidhāne arthavādaḥ’) explains it but Kumārila differs from him. Sāyaṇa and others explain it (e. g. the word [[* tridhā’)|‘tridhā’)]] with reference to Mantra, Brāhmaṇa and Kalpa, but when this verse was first proclaimed there must [[bave|have]] been no Brāhmaṇas and Kalpasūtras. In several cases the Nirukta gives the adhidaivata (physical) and adhyātma (metaphysical or spiritual) or adhiyajña and adhyātma as in Nir. X. 26 (on [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. [[82.2|82. 2]] [[Viśvakarmā|‘Viśvakarmā]] vimanā), Nir. XI. 4 [[( on|(on]] [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] X. 85. 3, [[adhiyajõa|adhiyajña]] and [[adhidaiyata|adhidaivata]] [[meanings ),|meanings),]] Nir. XII. 37 (on Vāj. S. [[34.55|34. 55]] ‘sapta [[rsayah’,|ṛṣayaḥ’,]] both adhidaivata and [[adhyātma ),|adhyātma),]] Nir. [[XII, 38 (On|XII. 38 (On]] Atharva X. 8. 9 [[Tiryag-bilas-oamasa &o.’|‘Tiryag-bilaś-camasa &c.’]] both adhidaivata and [[adhyatma).|adhyātma).]] In [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] (I. 164) verses 11-13 and 48 contain a very imaginative and poetic description of the year, the seasons, months and total days and nights.

In recent years [[Sri|Śrī]] Aurobindo in ‘Hymns to the mystic Fire’ (translated in the esoteric sense, [[1946 )|1946)]] and his ardent and devout disciple Sri. T. V. Kapali Sastry (in ‘[[Rgbhāṣya bhūmika|Ṛgbhāṣya-bhūmikā]] in Sanskrit and its English translation, Pondicherry [[1952 )|1952)]] have started a theory about the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] mantras which must be briefly stated and examined. [[Śri|Śrī]] Aurobindo first intended to publish an edition of the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] with a word by word [[Construction|construction]] and an English translation. But he gave up that idea on account of other [[pro-occupations|pre-occupations]] and rests content in the above book with the text and translation of about 230 verses in all from the 1st, 2nd and 6th [[mandalas|maṇḍalas]] of the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] and a foreword of 48 pages in which he propounds his theory. While [[P986]] this was being written by the present writer, [[Śri|Śrī]] Aurobindo’s large work of 634 pages [[on|‘on]] the Veda’ published in 1956 reached his hands. Over 60 hymns are dealt with in this big book and 283 pages are devoted to the elaboration of his theory first [[out lined|outlined]] in the brief work of [[Sri|Śrī]] Aurobindo mentioned above and reiterated with greater emphasis. On page 9 of the work of 1956 he states “the ritual system recognized by Sāyaṇa may stand, the naturalistic sense discovered by European [[scholar sbip|scholarship]] may, in its general conceptions be accepted, but behind there is always the true and still hidden secret of the veda [[-|—]] the secret words which were spoken for the purified in soul and the awakened in knowledge’. In this work also he sticks to the meaning of [[yta|ṛta]] as truth and on p. 84 translates [[‘ptam’|‘ṛtam’]] as [[‘Truth-conscious nass’,|‘Truth-consciousness’,]] when in his first work he translated [[‘fta-cit’|‘ṛta-cit’]] as [[Truth-con sciousness’.|Truth-consciousness’.]] Instead of comparing the several hundred passages where the word [[ṣta|ṛta]] occurs in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] he sticks to his own rendering which is unacceptable to most scholars and makes a [[Very|very]] perfunctory attempt at finding the correct meaning. One should like to have some clear examples of the distinction between Light and Consciousness in modern times and also in the Veda. So far as the present writer knows, in the language of ancient symbolism Consciousness is identified with Light. In the recently published book he deals with [[legs|less]] than [[it th|1/18th]] of the total hymns [[(1017 or 1028 )|(1017 or 1028)]] of the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda.]] Readers are asked to subscribe to his views although he [[condesconds|condescends]] to translate in the first work less than [[oth|1/40th]] of the total number of verses in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] and hardly ever enters into a discussion about the meaning of words like [[fta.|ṛta.]] [[Śri|Śrī]] Aurobindo [[( Foreword|(Foreword]] [[p. III)|p. III.)]] [[concodes|concedes]] that Sāyaṇa does not reject the spiritual authority of the Veda and that [[sāyana|Sāyaṇa]] does not deny that there is a higher truth contained in the [[rks.|ṛks.]] He further says (IX) that we must take seriously the hint of Yāska ([[Śri|Śrī]] Aurobindo does not quote the [[Nirukta.|Nirukta,]] but probably he has in mind Nir. I. 20 [[sākṣātkrtadharmāna|‘sākṣātkṛtadharmāṇa]] [[pgayo|ṛṣayo]] babhūvuḥ’). Then he proceeds to state (XVII) that many whole hymns of the Veda bear on their face a mystic meaning and that the [[rṣis|ṛṣis]] (p. XIX) for the sake of secrecy resorted to double [[meaninga,|meanings,]] a device easily manageable in the Sanskrit language. This is a hypothesis which cannot at all be accepted and is no more than [[more|mere]] conjecture. The Vedic mantras were composed thousands of years ago, when all persons among whom the [[gages|sages]] moved must have spoken the same language, though generally not [[80|so]] polished and poetical as that of the mantras and they were not addressed to men like the moderns whose [[P987]] every day thoughts, surroundings and languages are entirely different. Both the Master and the disciple (Mr. Kapali Sastry) cause confusion (or, may be, are themselves [[confused )|confused)]] by [[sup posing|supposing]] that the difficulties of the modern students of mantras did exist even at or near the time of the mantras. The most sublime thought of the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] is that there is only one Spirit behind the various gods, Indra, Mitra, Varuṇa, Agni, Yama, Mātariśvan, that originally there was only that One, there was no day and night, no death and immortality. [[Sri|Śrī]] Aurobindo himself describes (p. XXXII) [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] I. 164. 46 and X. [[129.2|129. 2]] as [[the|‘the]] summit of the [[vedio|vedic]] teaching.’ This view of the one Entity is also illustrated in [[Rg.|Ṛg.]] VIII. 58. [[%|2]] ‘one and the same Agni is kindled in many places, the one Sun enters the whole world and becomes many; the one Dawn illumines all this (physical) world, One became all this [[( assumes|(assumes]] various forms)’. No secrecy was observed about this [[fundamontal|fundamental]] truth and it was proclaimed in mantras that can be understood even by an ordinary man of to-day who knows a little Sanskrit. Because we of these recent centuries cannot understand some mantras, that does not mean that the ancient seers were guilty of a subterfuge and purposely composed mantras with two meanings. They might have indulged in the pastime of two meanings (express and metaphorical) in a few cases as [[postic|poetic]] [[devices.1612|devices.]]170 It is no fault of the sages, if we cannot understand their meaning, just as a blind man, who does not see a wooden post and comes to grief by dashing against it, should not blame the post, but should blame himself; we are to blame ourselves and should not foist upon the ancient sages a stratagem created by our own imagination or lack of [[know ledge|knowledge]] (Nir. I. [[16 ).1613|16).]]171

[[P988]] [[Śri|Śrī]] Aurobindo admits (p. XXXIII) that there are some key words in the [[Rgveda|Ṛgveda]] such as [[sta,|ṛta,]] kratu, [[gravas,|śravas,]] ketu and that [[elabo rate|elaborate]] work would have to be done to fix the meanings of such [[worde.|words.]] But he does not undertake a study of the key words (each of which except ‘ketu’ occurs hundreds of times in the [[Rgveda)|Ṛgveda)]] by comparison of [[Rgvedic|Ṛgvedic]] passages and considering the light [[shod|shed]] on these words by the other [[Samhitās|Saṁhitās]] and the Brāhmaṇas.
